The third leg is in the **schedule office at Stony Ford**, a long low building with a stove in the middle of it, and **Ren Shu** tells her before she asks that a reader is the only person in the building who stands in both the contractor's book and the office's, and that she is neither, so she does not stand in it while it is read. He reads to the room and not to her. **She is in the room for the whole hour and is not in the middle of it.** Eleven people write while a man reads and cannot all be listening, so they are not; they catch what they catch. **Her name and her hours come up at about a third of the way through and she does not see the number and cannot check it and will not be told it.** She works out in about nine seconds that she could walk to the stand and read the wedge and does not, because a number she read herself would be worth nothing to her — a relicensing needs a schedule, not a figure a woman saw once. **The countersignature goes on in an office's hand and not a man's**, on a sheet she will not hold again, with a line of office-name above the seal that she does not read and does not ask for. **The bar a registrar wrote was not an obstacle; it was a description of where she was standing.** **Discovery.** |
| 202 | Where It Leaks | Day 267, the worst ground on nine days. The ring is a foot of it on the low side of the worst stretch: grey, wet all the way down, takes a boot exactly as much as it takes a stone, and the whole of working it is putting your weight over the pull. **Ten men have been on it before her and every one of them has a way of walking it, and she has not.** **She clears forty yards of four hundred at a body that could have carried a sledge-load in either hand, and the heap she leaves dries in an hour and looks exactly like the ground it came from — which is why nobody from outside has ever caught anybody.** It came back in three days because the whole slope above it was not done in three seasons, and **the drift does not come up out of the ring; the ring is where it collects.** The girl of nineteen does the same eight yards in eight minutes and says nothing. **She says not one word about the drift to the foreman or the tender and does not offer to fix a schedule**, because what would come out of that conversation is *no, ma'am*, which is how a place says a thing about itself. **She is the strongest body on that course and the least useful person on it, because strength has never once been the shortage.** **Cost.** |
| 203 | Luo Ping | Days 267–268, in a hut at the back of the camp where the week's days get written into somebody else's book. **He is here and he is at work**, thirty-nine, thin wrists, a nib with a flat on it, and he tells her what he is before she asks: **a schedule-man, sixteen years, four or five offices in a season, sent in and paid by the day, and nobody in sixteen years has ever asked him whether a date ought to have gone in.** She spends two ration and gets two true answers and stops. She does not ask whether he was in a counting room in Lowwater, and does not tell him what the date was against, or that there is a licence with her name on it travelling with a bounty for her head on the back of it. **She does not tell him she knows about the household either — eleven days west there is a woman and a boy of eleven who do not know what their husband did for money — because saying it would be a way of making him small.** **A schedule-man is a trade and not a person; there are thousands, every office in nine counties has four or five, and she could have got here without finding him at all.** **The claim stands, the date is not torn off, Hou Sanliang is not met.** **Loss.** |
| 204 | The Last Cart West | Day 268, the day before the sixteenth. **Ma Sheng reads the west-going line out of the board in under a minute, because she is standing in front of the board and he keeps one — he does not ask why, and does not say she should go or should not.** Eighteen days east and back; the last carrier went west eleven days ago with the month on it; the next is a man who runs his own book, will not leave this week, and takes no passengers. **The reason she is nine days from a county counter is thirty coppers and a seat at a bridge, and that answer was given to her by her own purse four days ago and not by the road today.** And underneath, unargued: **she had known since the first of the month and had not counted the sixteenth** — not because the road was hard, not because the tin was thin, not because a county wrote something on a bar in the ordinary hand. **There are four separate days in that road where she could have turned round at a shoulder and had the morning four miles early.** The tin is gone. She spends one ration saying the date out loud, once, to nobody within forty feet. **New danger.** |
| 205 | Member Absent | Day 269, the sixteenth. **The county's monthly member sheet comes round with the carriers, because a county member sheet travels with the work the way a roster travels on a line.** It is not addressed to her and is not brought to her; it is on the trestle with the day's other paper until somebody turns it round. **Four columns, and the third and the fourth are not joined.** The sixteenth is ruled, her name is there, and in the attendance column in the hand that writes *Member present* are the county's own two words: **MEMBER ABSENT.** Beside it, in a contractor's hand in ink two days old, **nine days of her hours in order with dates on them, every one of them good — and not one hour of them has anything to do with the two words in the column next to them.** Ma Sheng tells her, unasked, in the ordinary voice, that **any person standing on the course may write a word in the reason column and that it does not take the attendance out; it only says why.** And **the reason column is the column her hours go in** — in the only book in the world that can certify an hour she worked out here, read in order with dates by an office past the boundary, on the strength of which she is about to be relicensed. **She has a true seven-word reason in her mouth for about four seconds and does not say it.** She does not ask for anything to be held or borrowed or said for her, and she does not thank him. **Then she stands up in daylight in front of people and works out that nobody stopped her: she did not count it, and there were four days on the road when she could have turned round, and she refuses out loud in her own head the version in which anything was taken out of her hands.** Nine men in a cold room nine days west are named in her own head as the cost and she cannot tell them. **It is permanent, separate from her hours, and not payable in hours. Irreversible decision — the batch's midpoint and the volume's.** |
| 206 | Underneath Her | Days 270–275, six days. She clears the ring and the ring fills and the ring gets cleared, and is paid six coppers a day and is not hungry. **The thing that went wrong is that she is cold in a place that has nothing to do with the weather**: a cold two inches under the breastbone, there at the fire and under three blankets and in the afternoon when eleven men are taking their coats off. **She is not short of breath in any new way and she is never going to be able to be full and warm at the same time again, and Condensing made her full and Foundation is putting a base under it** — and a base is a thing later techniques go on top of, so nothing she has already spent on technique can be put back on it, and she cannot tell whether what she feels is the schedule or her. **Two of the six days have nothing in them at all**, and that is what the ration going down by itself looks like. The crew is nine days west and nothing is going either way, and she works out that she has never in her life been so thoroughly outside a set of rooms. Ma Sheng brings a second blanket without a word and she does not thank him, because thanking him would be thanking him for noticing. **The year is touched once, at low volume, in one paragraph: the account is a purchase, it is still carrying, and there is not one line in it that says settled.** **Emotional consequence.** |
| 207 | Foundation One | Day 276. **The last of the ten days and the schedule went up on the seventh, and she was not in the room** — she had gone in once on the sixth and left after four minutes, because there is nothing in that room a woman in a doorway can do. The foreman brings the relicensed licence to the trestle himself and squares it and says nothing. **The front of it reads Foundation, stage one, and there is a ceiling above nine on the front of a document where there had been nothing above nine anywhere in the world for four months.** The back is the back: seven conditions untouched and the eight hundred jin the seventh and last. **She orders the announcement in the ordinary way and it goes about two hundred yards into a field.** Nine counties is what is heard and **nine counties have heard nothing this year**; four is what is written and the four books that would carry it are nine days west and have not been told. **It is not a failure: announcements are heard inside the bound and go no further, and there is a room and she is not in it, and nobody out here has ever heard one.** She does not attempt to explain any of it to anybody. She says *do not tell me about the ring tomorrow* and the argument goes on. **The cost is four weeks of a body that has never been full and cold at the same time**, and **every hour that bought it was read out of a book she was not shown, and if he had been wrong by an hour nobody in the world would ever know.** She does not find it funny. **She finds it correct, and being correct is worse.** **Revelation.** |
| 208 | The Counterfoil | Days 277–280, the last day of the batch. **A counterfoil is the stub a worker keeps when a wage-book pays** — the date, the hours, the stretch, the rate, the contractor's mark, and a painted mark of the stretch on the corner. **It is the only object in the world that can certify an hour she worked out there**: not a county seal, not a licence, not a certified copy obtainable with a stamp and a reason, and nobody in nine counties has ever seen one. She takes four and keeps them in the oilcloth above the plate with three dead weight tickets. **And the price column is the reason column and the hours go in it**, and she knows it now for certain and in her own hand. **She is relicensed above Condensing nine, in debt of an attendance that cannot be paid for in hours, working on a line of work kept by hand by people who are right, and holding the only book in the world that can say she was there** — all five of it, on four days' stubs. She asks the foreman the only question she asks in four weeks, and it is not about the work: **have you walked it.** He has not this season and most men have not, and **nine days** is written on the map on the back of a board in a room, and **she has seen three hundred yards of it.** She is not going west, and she had been thinking about it for eleven days and stopped. **And nobody on that course asked her a single question in four weeks**, which is the first place she has stood in where anybody wanted her hours and nothing else, and she says that once, in her own head, and attaches no lesson to it. **Decision.** |

### Batch 0003 (ch 209–222) — the line walked, and the people who live on it. WRITTEN, 28,568 words over fourteen chapters after the review repair, average ~2,041.

**Four bracketed layer lines in fourteen chapters, at ch 211, 214, 217 and 220, and zero in the other ten.** Foundation 1 to Foundation 5 across fourteen chapters, four stages, four costs, none of them the same thing. Day 281 to day 376.

| Ch | Title | Summary |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 209 | The Board | Days 281-283, the last three days of the month the road shut on. The man with the broken thumbnail is four days out at the far end of the reach and readings come in anyway, so **Cui Bao puts her on the board**: two planks on end props under an eave, a margin of about sixty painted marks, and a field ruled across, one line for a day, one cell per stretch. **A man comes in at the second hour and says *the whole of it* and she writes two hundred and forty in that cell, and a hole in that board is visible.** At the sixth hour the foreman reads it with a thumb and eleven men get up and go out. **Nobody upstream of that board exists.** A day's line takes her **eleven minutes where the tender takes four**, so the readings go up late on two days in three. **Nobody is rude, nobody is cruel, nobody offers to take a line off her, and nothing about it is ever mentioned again** — a place where people are paid by the day does not stop the line for a slow hand. She stands at it at the fourth hour for three mornings and copies the shape of a day onto the back of a dead weight ticket. **On the third morning the tender reads a line she wrote and does not change it, and that is the entire review.** Cui Bao is right about the schedule, the tender is right about the drift, the girl of nineteen is right about the pace; **she argues none of it and is given no answer.** **At the far end of the board, past the last mark anybody on the course attends, there is one more column — narrower, separately ruled, no heading she can read — and nothing on this course fills it, and it is not empty because it is new.** Discovery. |
| 210 | The Carrier | Days 284-295, the carrier's return about the third. **Ma Sheng reads the west-going line out of his board for the second time**: the carrier is coming back east about the third of the month, and after him a man of the lower valley who runs his own book and leaves when he leaves — **and after him nothing is written for the rest of the season.** That is not a shut road; it is a road with no times on it. **The county's round comes round**: the member's sheet with MEMBER ABSENT in it went west after the sixteenth and came back east with a carrier who had nothing to do with her, and it carries eleven days of her hours in the column beside it and a foot and a half of blank paper. **Underneath it, a slip of county paper about two inches by three: the district's answer, come down the stair. The empty space has a date in it and one office word — *Held*.** It means the paper is in a room and nothing has been done to it. **She heard a second meaning in it, she is the only person within nine days who could, and she told nobody and did not put it into a sentence with a verb even in her own head.** She does not go: there is no money and she would not have gone, and the point is that the option has stopped existing. She stands at the trestle on the morning he goes anyway, at the fourth hour, because that is where she is at the fourth hour. She spends one ration asking him the carrier's schedule and gets back the thing she was given for nothing on the first. **The specific and unromantic terror of a woman who has just made herself the sort of person who lives where she is.** Irreversible decision. |
| 211 | What Six Coppers Buys | Day 296, the thirteenth, twenty days after the first Foundation. A store at the back of the camp — a plank on two barrels, a man of sixty who sells oil, salt and lamp. **Four pounds of rendered oil, a half-peck of quicklime, two hanks of hempen line, a length of good ox-hide and a round of beeswax**, all five **weighed and measured in front of her, one at a time, by a man who is not interested and who does not charge her for a minute and a half of listening.** **It is thirty coppers: five days of the six.** She pays out loud in coin at the plank on the twelfth. **She got the thirty by not putting her share into the cook-fire for five days, and from that day the fire is not lit out of her share — about eleven cold days in two stretches, and after the first night being cold is weather and not a decision.** She buys her own bread at the store and takes herself out of the fire for the rest of the batch. **[The first bracketed line of the batch]** — it names her, names Foundation, names the second stage and stops; about two hundred yards into a field. **Nine counties is what is heard and nine counties heard nothing; four is what is written and the four books are nine days west.** **She is not hungry, notices it on the day of the stage, and does not have time for the thing underneath it.** Cost. |
| 212 | The Courtesy | Days 297-299, the attendance falling on the sixteenth. **MEMBER ABSENT for the second time**, in the county's own two words, in the hand that writes *Member present*, in a column that is not joined to the column her hours go in, with eleven days of good hours beside it and a foot and a half of blank paper in the reason column. **And Ma Sheng does not offer it again** — he reads her line, stands there with his hands at his sides, and goes and writes a man's hours against a stretch. **Three men had been arguing at that trestle for the half hour before he came, about whether the thirty-nine reads on the nineteenth, and she stood inside that talk waiting for a fourth sentence that did not come.** She works out inside about a second that the offer at ch 205 was never a rule and never a standing arrangement: **it was a man, not asked, saying a thing he was entitled to say once**, and saying it twice would have turned a kindness into a term of something. **A person who is owed a kind word is not owed it twice, and she was not even a woman to him; she was a hand on a board.** And the part that costs her is that **she had gone on working on the strength of the knowing**, building a floor out of about nine seconds of a man being decent, and it came down in nine seconds and was still standing. No ration spend. **Emotional consequence.** |
| 213 | The Noise In The Ground | Days 300-315, sixteen days, on the low side of the thirty-nine and the forty. **A bar under two feet of it makes a noise, and the bar stops and the ground does not** — low, under the arm, no edge on it, about as long as it takes to count eleven. **Eleven men and a girl of nineteen have an ordinary working word for it — a stretch that has got loud** — and they say it as a fact about ground, and the girl is right about which stretch is worse. **The difference between her and the girl of nineteen is not strength and nobody on that course has ever thought it was.** Over sixteen days she keeps **what the board says against what she remembers of nine counties hearing, in order, over four years** — the only arithmetic in the world she can do: **the loud stretches are nearest the inside end, the worst of them is the worst-worked, and the quietest ground on nine days is at the far end, which is where there is nothing to announce because there is nobody there.** She says it to herself once, in one plain sentence, builds no theory on it, **tells nobody and does not tell him** — he cannot read a board and the only thing he could have done with it was spend out of her own column. **It cannot be proved, because the only control in the world is herself.** One ration spent and it buys eleven words about a man who has left, and she knows it while he is saying it. **They are right about the schedule and right about where it collects and she watched the proof arrive in the only form proof arrives in; the tenth part is hers and she cannot show anybody a line of it.** Revelation. |
| 214 | Four Counts | Day 316, the third, twenty days after the second Foundation. **Short breath goes to four counts on the flat, on the line and on a grade, and three is not coming back** — she says it once, out loud, in a hut, to nobody, and does not say it again in the batch. The three was not a reserve or a size; it was the count between one thing and the next, the walk from the bunk to the wire and back, and she had been quietly spending it since the thaw. **[The second bracketed line.]** She tests herself twice on the fourth and writes four lines on a dead weight ticket: flat four, grade four, ring four, flat four — **and left hand, write: unchanged, eleven minutes with the flat of a second finger**, put there on purpose, in case there was ever a morning when she needed to know which of the two hands was the one that had gone. **She is stronger on that ground than she has ever been and worse at the work she is paid for, and both of those are true at the same time and there is nobody within nine days of road to tell her which she is.** **The girl of nineteen stops at the end of her eight yards and tells her she is stopping on every pull, which is the first true thing anybody has said to her about her body in four years, and she gives her no answer because there is none she can give.** Cost. |
| 215 | The Man With A Notebook | Days 317-329, thirteen days. **BATCH MIDPOINT.** He stands on high ground where a man stands to see the ring, the board and the trestle without being in any of them, and writes everything down, and **buys nothing, threatens nothing, touches her, enters nothing, and says in the same breath that he has not asked her a question and is not going to start.** **Why he walked nine days east:** a field man of a Warden's office cannot unmake a System inside a bound; that is what a **Wall writ** is for, and a writ is issued by the line's own people to somebody who lives on it, and she is not one of them and the line has no roll of everybody who stands on it. **What the line can do is close its own working ground against a person it does not carry** — nine years old, used twice, right both times. **And he does not want it executed.** He wants the answer pending, because a pending answer is a question somebody above him has to answer and he has never once believed the answer would be the right one. **He says that out loud, in the ordinary voice, in front of the woman it is being done to, and it is worse than a threat.** She asks him one thing — whether anybody comes west out of that country — and pays a ration for an answer the board had already given her for nothing. **On the eleventh she watches the foreman tell him the rule at a wire and watches him write it down, from forty feet, and understands in the time it takes to put a board down that it is over, and does not go and stand in front of him.** The month goes west in one sentence. Not fear: **the adult humiliation of being told by an honest man that the only thing that can be done about her is done by strangers who have never met her and who will be right.** Revelation. |
| 216 | The Last Column | Days 330-337, the seventeenth to the twenty-fourth. **The board is two planks and she had only ever looked at one of them.** At the far end of the far plank, past the last mark anybody attends, is a column: narrower, separately ruled, no heading she can read, **filled by a strip of the same cloth off a cart from the far end of the working reach, going up for nine years**, and **nobody on this course can read it and nobody has ever asked**; the tender's whole account is *it comes off the cart and it goes up and it is not for us.* **The cart is not identified on that course.** She can read the frame and not one word inside it — dates at the left, spacing that is not the spacing of readings, a figure that is not a measurement of ground — **and one line is dated the thirteenth of this month, the day she took the second Foundation, in a form none of the nine years around it is in.** She is the only person within nine days who can tell that line from a reading, **she tells nobody that she has told the difference, and she does not say out loud what the line is.** She comes at it sideways for three days — counts about eleven hundred lines and stops, works out the cart comes alone about once in ten days, finds the strip torn against a rule and not cut. **Her hand was on the Ledger that evening and she did not order it** — one sentence, no dialogue, no price, nobody told — **and what she went over afterwards was not the wanting but how quick it was, and that the not-deciding is free.** New danger. |
| 217 | The Left Hand | Day 338, the twenty-fifth, twenty-two days after the third Foundation. **Not the heel — the heel went before the quarry and is not what this is about. What went is the order:** the wrist already turned, the fingers already closing, the turn before anybody thinks. Two years of rebuilding a hand with no heel into a working hand. **[The third bracketed line.]** **After it she can still hold, carry, pull and work, and the sequence is gone** — every grip built from the beginning, one at a time, in about a breath, before the bar lands. The distance the order has to travel is four inches. **The difficulty is not the delay; a bar will wait for a hand. The difficulty is that in the ring the turn cannot be built, because it has to be at the top of the pull.** She goes out at the fourth hour on the same morning with the hand she has got, and turns a bar over about four hundred times, building every one. **Grief, and underneath it envy: the girl of nineteen has the order and is nineteen and is not stupid and is not wrong about any part of it, and is not better at anything else.** **At the ninth hour a man she has never spoken to puts his bar down flat and asks her why she is stopping on every pull; she says her hand is slow, and he says his is slow too, and asks her nothing else.** She was the strongest body on the course and she has lost the ability to do the ordinaryest thing on it. It changed nothing about the wage-book. **The writing hand is unchanged and she wrote that on the ticket on purpose.** Loss. |
| 218 | Closing The Ground | Days 339-351. **BATCH CLIMAX.** **The line's own people close their course against her, lawfully, correctly, off page, and it is not the reader's victory either.** The decision was made at a table in a room at the west end, by the foreman, the tender and the man in the plain coat, **and the written reason is the Hunter's own referral, read out of his notebook by him, in an office's hand, in the ordinary voice, because a foreman asked a man with a notebook what the paper was and he answered him truthfully.** The referral says the thing in her head is unauthorised; it says it is carried by a licensee in good standing; **both are true, both are on the same paper, and the second is her own name.** She had nine days of knowing and did not get up and do anything about it, because there was nothing to get up and do. **It reached her on the eighth of a month at about the sixth hour at a trestle, in nine words, in the ordinary voice, and everybody carried on.** **She said nothing at all in her own defence.** There is no version of anything she could have said: she has four years of paper behind her and every argument she ever won she won with a form, and the form is about to be read correctly. **A foreman is not being careful and is not being cruel; he is doing the only thing he is built to do and she has no counter. Nobody stopped her and nothing was taken out of her hands: she is not the kind of person the ground closes on, and that is the whole reason it worked.** **What it costs them, in arithmetic and not in a speech:** the run is shorter, the contract is smaller, the board has a hole in it where a mark used to get a figure, nine men have nothing to be put on, and the girl of nineteen, who is somebody's daughter. One ration spent and it bought nothing at all. Climax / reversal. |
| 219 | Standing Off | Days 352-359. **The stretch she kept went back in three days**, faster than last time, and she watched it and she knows the four places where it takes a boot. **The men are slower in that ring than she was and they do not know it and never will.** **The cost to them on the ground and not in a speech:** nine men idle by the sixteenth because a run with a hole in it is a smaller run; one of them asks the foreman whether the stretch will come back and is told no, with no reason, because there is nowhere in that camp to put the reason. **The girl of nineteen is idle from the
eighth** — she worked the twenty-eight on the ninth and after that the run had four stretches on it and she is one hand and she is nineteen, and she comes and sits on the other side of the same cart wheel and asks whether the forty will be put on before the month turns, and is told no. **On the fourteenth she goes out past the wire with a bar, about two hundred yards, to the worst ground on nine days, where there is nobody because the ground is closed.** The boot goes. **She puts her weight over the pull. She does not fall, nothing breaks, nobody is hurt, nobody comes.** An hour and a half, and then back over the wire at the sixth hour and nobody saw it. **There is nothing on earth she can do with being strong.** She is jealous of nine men who are about to be measurably worse at a job she is not allowed to do, and being ashamed of it did not stop it. The month's sheet goes west in one sentence. **Emotional consequence.** |
| 220 | Struck Out | Day 360, the seventeenth, twenty-two days after the fourth Foundation. **A book is written at the start of a day and squared at the end of a run, and at about the second hour the foreman came out with it, squared it on the plank, ran a thumb down a column, and drew one line through her name with a pen off his belt — in the open, in daylight, with six men within thirty feet, and not at night and not in a room.** **The hours stand: every day from the fourth of the month before last to the eighth of this month is in that book in his own hand with dates on it, and none of it is touched. The rate stops. The book does not close and goes on being a book other people write in.** She does not ask him to do it later, because a book that keeps a name that has not been worked is a book that lies, and he would have had to have left a hole in the only continuous run of her hours in the world. **The Hunter was there, watched it, wrote it down afterward, and said nothing to her. His daughter asked him, ten feet off and in the open, what the line was, and he told her once and flat that the hours are in the book and stay in it, and she said *Oh* and went and got her bar, and nobody looked at Ning Yan while any of it happened.** **[The fourth bracketed line.] Foundation 5 the same week** — the two things are not connected, and she had two days to be certain of that, **and being certain was worse than not knowing, because if the stage had cost her the name she could have carried both as one thing.** **It is the first time in four years a piece of paper has been about her that was written by a person who does not know her.** And not defeat: **as long as her name was in that book it was the only book in the world with her name in it, and now there is a second possibility.** Cost. |
| 221 | The Second Book | Days 361-375. **THE BATCH'S IRREVERSIBLE DECISION.** The course is closed and she is the strongest body on nine days and **there is no version of a foreman's contract that carries a woman he has struck out.** Past the last stake there is a **sub-contractor** — a man who works the ground between the course and the far end, not answerable to the foreman, four men and sometimes six, a book of his own with four columns and a painted mark — **and his rate is five coppers and not six, and the difference is never explained and is not a mystery.** **He does not ask why the course closed. He was not there and nobody told him, and a man who runs work out past the last stake has never in his life asked a hired hand why she came.** He names **a stretch nobody is hired on** — the worst ground on nine days, on nobody's book, nobody's job — tells her not to be clever at it, and **she does not go on it and does not ask about it.** He gives her the only two things anybody in nine days can: the far side is ordinary country and nobody there is waiting for her; a carter will take a person out past the last stake for money; **and there is a woman out past the far end with one team, whose name is Wei Ran, who is twenty.** **She takes the work on the twentieth, in daylight, at the trestle, with her name said out loud for the second time on that ground.** **Two books in two hands now hold her name, neither answerable to the other, and the hours split so that neither carries a continuous run — the exact instrument the whole relicensing was built out of. She took it apart with her own mouth and nobody had to be persuaded.** She does the arithmetic on the first morning and **does not do it out loud.** **A colder bargain than the last one and no shame in it at all: five coppers from a man who does not know what she is, for the right work, by a woman who knows exactly what she is. It is the best deal she has been offered in four volumes.** Irreversible decision. |
| 222 | The Far End | Day 376, the third. **THE BATCH'S IMAGE.** Five coppers to a carter, in the morning, in front of nobody, and she is set down in a field a quarter of a mile past the last stake and picked up at the seventh hour because he usually is. **The drift is thin, there is no ring, and there is not a bar's worth of work in fifty yards.** A bar into six inches of it **does not ring**, and it made the sound a bar makes and stopped when the bar stopped. **She stood in the quietest ground on nine days for about a minute and a half, and the reason she worked out sixteen days ago with a bar in her hands was standing under her boots: the quiet is where there is nobody.** **The place where the ring has been living in her head for five weeks was empty, and the quietest thing in that field was her own noise.** A woman with a team comes over: **Wei Ran, twenty, mortal-born, a carter who runs one team out past the far end of the working reach and back.** Asked one direct question — *how did you get through* — **she said *I did not*, and went on loading, and did not elaborate and did not ask her back.** Then she says the correct thing about the line as a fact and not as comfort: **the drift is thin out here because nobody works it, and it is thin here and not thick up there for a reason that has nothing to do with the ground** — and that half of it had never occurred to Ning Yan, who had thought for four weeks that a fact about the drift eleven men could not work out was hers alone. **Two people who went through, sitting on a flat cart nine feet apart with their boots off, and neither of them said one word about either of the things they have in common.** One ration spent and it buys nothing at all and the chapter does not say what he said. She had learned that the drift is loud where the most has been said and cannot prove a line of it and has told nobody. **And there is no word within nine days of road for either of the two things she has learned this season: the noise, and the quiet.** Past the ground she was standing on there is a stretch nobody is hired on, named a week ago, not entered, not worked and not cleared. **Revelation / image.** |


### Batch 0004 (ch 223–238) — crossing, and what it costs. WRITTEN IN FULL, 31,490 words over sixteen chapters, average ~1,968. **ch 223–230 in the first half and ch 231–238 in the second, and ch 238 is the last chapter of Volume 4.**

| Ch | Title | Day / days | Summary |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 223 | Unpaid Ground | Days 377–385, the 4th to the 12th | Opens the batch. **Nine mornings at the fourth hour into a sub-contractor's book with four columns and a painted mark, at five coppers, bread bought at the store and no share in the cook-fire.** The stretch is not hers — it carries somebody else's painted mark and somebody else's figure — and **she is the slowest hand on it**, at two feet of it in one movement on a ring where eleven men cannot follow her. **The order is gone out of her left hand and every grip is built from nothing before the bar lands, so she keeps a count: eleven holds that hold to the end of a stroke out of about sixty tried, written on the back of a dead weight ticket at eleven minutes a line, and she does not write the lost ones down.** A man of about twenty mending a boot explains the difference between the ground at her end of the run and the ground at the far end of it — *like a bucket of something wet, and you are pulling on the whole of it* — and says he will not be working that end again, and asks her nothing. **The arithmetic of two books comes out the same on the second morning as on the first, and she does not do it out loud: the only instrument the world has ever given her is a continuous run of her hours in one book in one hand in order with dates, and she took it apart herself on purpose eleven days earlier.** Ten coppers turn over without building; five in the morning and five out in the evening; a tin gone since an evening four months back. **He names the bad flat again in the ordinary way and tells her not to be clever at it and does not say it twice.** She stands at the trestle looking at the rise and does not walk over it, and writes *she wanted to* on a ticket and does not write it again. **Discovery.** |
| 224 | What The Ear Can Tell | Day 386, the 13th | **[BRACKET 1] Foundation 6, twenty-six days from the fifth, at her own order, in the ordinary way.** It announces and stops. **What hears it is a course with nine idle men, a girl of nineteen, a foreman and a tender, about nine hundred yards inside the wire, across about two hundred yards of open ground from the trestle.** All nine men look up inside about the same part of a second; **the girl of nineteen does not, and is the only one who does not** — she goes on working for about six seconds, then stops with the bar in the air and looks down her own stretch, and there is nothing out that way. The foreman comes out, looks up, looks down the run for as long as it takes a man to decide there is nothing to find, and goes back in. **Then a hand goes flat on a board** — not on a line, not on a column — and takes itself off again. **THE COST, NAMED ONCE: the sound in their ears becomes a line on a board, and the people on the line can tell the difference between a stage and a bill.** A board carries dates and stretches and hours and prices, and a sound goes into none of them. She puts a bar into the ring on purpose and it rings for about as long as it takes to count eleven. **Cui Bao comes out past the wire in the afternoon and says the whole of it — the referral was correct, he acted on it inside a week, the run is a fortnight shorter, nine men are idle and his daughter is idle, he heard her stage go and wrote none of it down, and he says he would do all of it again on any working morning — and does not want her to thank him for it.** **The anger is not at them: every person who is right about the ground is right about her too, and there is no version of being right that leaves her name in either book with a run in it.** **Cost.** |
| 225 | The Blank Reason | Days 388–393, the 15th to the 20th; the attendance on the 16th | **Pei, about sixty, a carrier with a coat repainted twice, NEW, brings the county's member sheet round past the last stake with a cart of oil and salt.** He is the hand that writes the attendance column and he says once that the reason column after it **is not his to fill, because it travels down the same road as her hours**, and that he will not say it again until he has to write in it. **He offered the word once himself, on an earlier round of his, and a decent man at a trestle inside the wire offered it once before that, and she took it neither time, and no man has offered it a second time.** He does not ask her a question about it, because in a year he does not want to be a man who asked one. **She does not say the reason aloud at all.** It is on the page once and not in her mouth: **the reason column is for hours, and a road put in it would be the last thing an office reads about her before it decides what she is.** On the sixteenth he writes **MEMBER ABSENT** in the hand that writes *member present* and the reason column goes west **a foot and a half wide and blank**, for the third time, and **that one sentence is the whole of the entry.** He goes west that afternoon with the strip inside his coat and she walks a hundred and forty yards with him and lets the cart go on over the rise. **There is no date west on any board after that man for the rest of the season**, and no carrier is coming back either. About five coppers, bread at the store, and **the sixth stage's cost felt once more, three days back: eleven men heard a thing go up out of the ground at the ninth hour and not one of them could write it down.** **She had learned to work inside a blank column going west and the learning is now three columns wide and still blank.** Emotional consequence. |
| 226 | Bread At The Store | Days 394–403, the 21st to the 30th | **Ten days is fifty coppers of work and she had about ten coppers, and she works the arithmetic on the first day and then stops, because a woman who counts at a bread counter does not stop counting.** There is **one store between the last stake and the cart turn** and a woman behind the counter who has never asked her anything. **She has thought about the fact that a woman comes out of that line without a pot** and says so without asking, and puts the bread down on the other side of the salt, and tells her to stop standing in the middle of the floor, and does not call it an offer. Eleven hours a day on a worked stretch with four men, and **the content of those ten days is that she can do four times what any of them can do at any moment and has to choose to do exactly what they are doing.** **Two true things at once: she is the strongest body on nine days and not close, and she is worse at the work than she was at the fourth of the month and going to stay worse for the rest of the season — the base is heavier and colder and slower, and she is paid five coppers for the second of those.** **The cold has no source.** For four years every cold she stood in was done to her by a person; this one is the stone of her running cold at four counts, and she is colder at the fire than in a hut. **On the twenty-ninth she walks a quarter of a mile past the trestle and stands on the rise for eleven minutes and does not go over it, and understands in one plain sentence why the ground is quiet — there is nobody out there — and does not say the sentence out loud.** Emotional consequence. |
| 227 | The Stretch With No Hire | Days 404–411, the 1st to the 8th | **A load of stone goes past the cart turn because the turn is the short way and the road round is two days, and she walks beside the handcart because she is there.** Forty minutes out, a third of it on ground that belongs to somebody; the rest is **not on anybody's plank.** **THE BAD FLAT: three-quarters of a mile with no mark, no stake and no board at any point on it — the only place on nine days where a person can see where ground stops being anybody's job is where the ground stops being anybody's job.** A foot and a bit on the low side all the way, thickest where the work was worst. **No ring anywhere in it.** She goes down into the low side about a hundred yards in and stands in it for five minutes, and the drift holds a boot the way a ditch holds a man, and the danger is not the depth — **it is that the ground does not tell you which way it has moved while your weight was on it** — and at about the eighth turn the low side goes out from under the outside of her foot and she is on one knee in it before she has decided to be. **Two men are in the ground four inches deep and flat out with a bar laid across the both of them, drift closed over them, and the bar still there.** **No board, no name, no wage and no man who stands a stretch on it; two men and a day and a half to walk it.** **The shortage there was never strength — it is how long a person can be wrong in it and not go in**, and four counts is the whole of the difficulty. **The wheel ruts tell her the rest: four inches deep and nine years old on the high ground, shallow and one set and not recent through the flat — somebody went through once, then went round, and everybody has gone round since, and nobody has written the reason down because there is nothing on that road to write it on.** Eight days worked and **she is ashamed of the ground and jealous of it and they are the same thing.** New danger. |
| 228 | Three Days Missing | Days 412–414, the 9th to the 11th | **She goes out on the ninth at the fourth hour and is not at the trestle for three days.** It is not the bad flat — it is **the ground in between, two hundred yards past the last mark, on nobody's plank**, and the reason is the quiet: **on a worked stretch with a painted mark and a ring in the ground the base will not turn, and she has run four years on ground that talks.** She sleeps in her own hut because the trestle is not hers, works by hand with a bar, builds a hold and puts the weight over it and takes the weight off about forty times an hour, and is cut across the two places a bar holds a hand. **[BRACKET 2] Foundation 7 at about the fourth hour on the first of the three days, with her back against a rise three hundred yards past the last stake.** The sound arrives on a course nine hundred yards inside the wire and **does not carry a direction** — a board with a hole in it and a hand that does not know where to look. **THE COST, NAMED ONCE: three days no book can find, and eleven men who stand a stretch for nothing.** She comes in on the third evening and reads the foreman's board — **which stands at the wire on the course's own ground** — from forty feet: **three new lines dated the ninth, the tenth and the eleventh, a stretch in the stretch column in Cui Bao's hand, and no man against any of them** — a course with nothing to do cannot pay a man to keep a stretch and wrote it anyway because the stretch had to be kept. **They did not tell her and she went looking for that for about three days.** The girl of nineteen was out alone on the second of the three days, four hundred yards off, and **did not look in the direction of the rise.** Being carried by people who are right about her is worse than being refused by them. **The sub-contractor squares nothing and writes nothing and says *you want the tenth, the eleventh and the twelfth filled in* / *they are three days I did not work your stretch* / *that is what I said.***

⚠️ Ch 228's three missing days run **412–414**, not 412 alone as the card table gives, and **the board lines are dated the ninth, the tenth and the eleventh accordingly**; ch 229 opens on day 415. The four pinned stage days and the pinned attendance day are untouched. **Loss.** |
| 229 | The Sub-Contractor's Mark | Days 415–419, the 12th to the 16th | **Five days, and she does not try to put anything into the three empty dates, and it takes her about four minutes to decide and it is the first decision in a month she did not have to argue with anybody about.** He squares the book on the sixteenth, runs his thumb down three empty lines, **goes on**, and writes the figures beside the twelfth through the sixteenth. She asks whether he could have put hours in them. **He says no: if he puts hours in there and a man reads that book in six weeks he has a hole he cannot see, and a hole he cannot see is worse than one he can.** **And then: it is in my book and my book is true, and it is not my business what a hole in it costs a woman nine days of road away.** He tears off **the first counterfoil in her life in a second contractor's mark** and puts it on the plank face down and goes to the pot. **A mark nobody checks is worth what the man who makes it is worth — it goes in a coat, it is carried about by a person, and it is kept — and not one of the five can be compared with either book.** She lays all five out on her knee and reads them against each other like a clerk reading two columns; the dates come out clean and **not one thing on any of the five refers to the ninth, the tenth or the eleventh, and she writes those three days nowhere.** **Wei Ran passes once with a team and a load**: she would not get a better stretch, he would think about it for a day; **twenty coppers and a road, not a favour**; and the true thing about the flat — *I have taken two loads that way in four years and I went round both times, and everybody who works a line looks at ground nobody hires on about once a season and about half of them go.* **The seventh stage in the body: heavy, cold, and strong in a way that has stopped being useful** — a man at the trestle watches her put a bar over four times without shifting his feet and uses the word a man uses when he is looking at something he does not have to do. **A win immediately followed by its cost:** the seventh stage is held, and the price of it is three dates that will never carry a run, and she carries both. Emotional consequence. |
| 230 | Ledger For A Crossing | Days 420–426, the 17th to the 23rd. **BATCH MIDPOINT** | **The arithmetic is set down in full and none of it changes anything.** Two books, one of them ended two months back, the other with three empty dates in it, **and no stretch of continuous work in either on nine days of road.** Five stubs with five marks, **and a man at a desk would have to hold two books and five papers and decide which he believed, and there is no rule about hours that lets him do that and be honest.** A licence with a ceiling at Foundation the first stage that she has never touched. **And a quarter of a mile of ground past the cart turn that is on nobody's book.** **What does not follow from two books:** hours in order with dates do not cross a boundary no county paper crosses, and the only instrument there has ever been is one continuous run, and a run is a thing two books cannot make, and **she took them apart herself and has not asked for it to be put back together.** **One ration spend**, and it buys her this, in the ordinary voice: **“Say it and I hold it as long as you want it held.”** She answers **“Not for a stage.” · “No.” · “Not today.” · “You know the day.”** **The fear, admitted once:** not of the bad flat — of spending him on purpose. She did it once before, eight days out of ten, for a name, and has never been sure whether it was a purchase or a theft; **this time there is no name at the end of it, and what it comes out of is the foundation she is standing on while the foundation is still being built.** She looks at it and puts it down and picks it up four times, **and it is not a reason.** **The decision is taken and nothing is said:** she will hold the Ledger for the crossing, on the fifth of the next month, inside the turn of the base and not outside it. **The year is touched once, at low volume, and does not close: she goes on using him on purpose, it is not forgiveness, and the account carries to the end of the book.** She stands on the rise for nine minutes and goes back to bed. **And at about the seventh hour, across the ground between the trestle and that rise, a man in a plain coat stands at the course's trestle with his notebook shut against his side, not writing.** Decision. |

| 231 | The Price Before The Word | Days 427–433, the 24th to the 30th | **THE PRICE, STATED BEFORE THE WORD, ONCE, WITH NOTHING ATTACHED TO IT.** A man in a plain coat is on the high ground at the fifth hour on seven mornings out of seven and comes two hundred yards down to where a man stands to see the ring, the board and the plank without being in any of them, and writes. **He walks past the store at the cart turn every evening at the seventh hour, when she comes out of it with bread under her arm, and does not go in.** On the twenty-seventh he says the whole of it in one breath so that she does not have to spend a morning on it: **he has not bought anything, threatened her, touched her, entered anything or asked her a question, and he is not going to start.** **THREE LINES GO IN THE NOTEBOOK over the seven days and she never sees the page:** the course closed its own ground lawfully and correctly; the person in question was hired on the same day by a sub-contractor outside the wire at five against six and has put her hours in his book in order with dates; she is working past the last stake on a stretch carrying somebody else's mark and **has asked for nothing since except bread.** She works the seven days, loses four holds and builds seven, writes them on the back of the ticket at eleven minutes a line, and has about five coppers on the twenty-fourth and about five on the thirtieth. **She spends one of three marks on the twenty-sixth and it buys her the price and nothing else: “One day out of the three you are keeping.”** He does not tell her not to do it, does not tell her to do it, does not attach an explanation to it, and stops the way a layer stops. **Two left, and two is the whole price, and after the crossing there is nothing of him left that she can spend on purpose, and the only thing in the world that puts any of it back is work.** **The part of it that was not a threat:** he said four months back that he does not want his own order executed, and he is attending the execution of it and keeping the minutes, **and she wanted him to lie once — that was the want, and she had not known she had it until the twenty-eighth — so that she could be angry at a person instead of at a procedure.** He cannot lie and he is losing and that is worse than a threat. **Five days to the fifth, one mark left, and a quarter of a mile of quiet ground that four sentences will not do.** New danger. |
| 232 | Hands In Two Books | Days 434–437, the 1st to the 4th | **She squares the two books before the crossing.** He turns the page back, runs his thumb down the date column without stopping, runs it down the hours column, squares the page with the heel of his hand, and **goes past three empty lines without stopping on them.** She lays the oilcloth out on the plank in daylight — five counterfoils in two marks, three dead weight tickets, the ration on the back of one — and checks **date against date, stretch against stretch, hours against hours, price against price,** at eleven minutes a line with the flat of a second finger. Then the other way, which takes longer and is the only way that counts. **WHAT A CLEAN SPLIT COMES TO: every hour she has worked since the seventeenth of a month two months back is in one book or the other. Not one in both. Not one in neither. That is the best split a person can have and it is worth nothing.** She writes it out on the third ticket in her own hand and then writes **a run is one hand in order with dates** underneath it and stops, because a thing written twice in the same hand in the same hour is a thing a person is arguing with herself about. **Three doors and all three shut at the plank, and she shut two of them herself and refused to be refused by anything.** She says the first one out loud once — *copy the hours out of the course's book into the back of yours* — and about nine seconds later he comes and gets a pot and says what he says: **“Twenty-two. Fifth of the month. Same as the rest.”** She does not ask again; a decent man does not offer a thing twice. On the third he tells her one true thing about the course unasked — **the man over the wire keeps a copy of everything he sends and does not keep a copy of what he keeps** — and does not say it again. **THE CALM, THE FIRST IN THE BATCH: she is not calm because anything got better. The attendance went west blank for the third time and is never coming back, the three empty dates are going to stay empty, and she has four fingers' worth of nothing on a hand nobody has asked anything of.** She is calm because **she has stopped — for four years she believed the paper would fix the rest, and there is one place within nine days of road where a book is kept by the people whose ground it is, and nobody there has ever refused anybody anything.** She does not order anything. She sleeps on it. Revelation. |
| 233 | The Blind Run | Days 438–439, the 5th and the 6th | **SHE HIRES ONTO THE STRETCH NOBODY IS HIRED ON.** He writes her down at the fourth hour as he writes everybody down, then shuts the book part way and names it in the ordinary way, the way a man names a neighbour's field, and tells her he is not going to warn her twice. She asks for it and does not argue for it once. **THREE TRUE THINGS:** there is nobody to hire on that ground and it is not on my book because it is not my work; **it pays two coppers and it goes in your hand and it does not go in mine**; and **if you go into that low side and it does not come up I will not be the man who stands a stretch on it, because there is no stretch and there is no board and there is nobody to stand it.** **Her name said out loud for the third time on that ground, in daylight, in front of four men at the pot and a man with a boot off his foot, and nobody persuaded her.** The man with the boot says he will come and look at it on the eleventh and will not go down into it. **WHAT IS ON IT, told by him in the present voice and in about four sentences:** the ground is listed; the layer lists jobs, not men; that one is on it because it is work nobody will take and nobody has finished; **and when somebody finishes it, it settles — and it does not settle into a county book and it does not settle into a man with a board at a trestle two hundred yards behind you.** **THE WORD IS SAID** at about the fifth hour on the fifth, standing on the ground she has stood on every evening for a month, with four men at a pot about eight feet behind her: **“One day out of the three I was keeping. Say it and hold it until the crossing is over.” · “I am holding it.”** **The price was said once on the twenty-sixth and neither of them says it again.** She has one mark left and does not spend it on this. **THE FIRST HOUR:** forty minutes out, ten of them on ground that is not a stretch, the low side eleven feet in, the bar going down a foot and stopping on nothing; a hold built from nothing in about as long as a man counts out a day's coppers; **two feet of it up in one movement with nobody standing anywhere near her to see it**; **four hours and four counts of breath inside the second one; and she wrote the day down at the top of the rise in one line that took eleven minutes, because nobody else is going to.** Bread for five coppers out of the two she earned and the five in her hand. Irreversible decision. |
| 234 | The Hour Without A Line | Day 440, the 7th | **FOUNDATION 8 IS SUPPRESSED AT HER OWN ORDER AND THIS CHAPTER CARRIES NO BRACKETED LAYER LINE AT ALL.** Twenty-eight days, counted off the back of a dead weight ticket since the ninth of the month before. She says the word in a hut with three boards in it the way she has said every one for two years — **and this time it did not go.** She noticed in about two seconds, because it has never once not gone, and she **did not tell him to.** **There was no moment in it where she decided.** She thought about saying it after the fact for about as long as a man counts out a day's coppers, and what stopped her was not pride and was not the account: **a stage is worth something to nine counties and it is worth nothing at all to the far end of the working reach, and there is one announcement in four years that a board two hundred yards behind her has ever taken, and she had been carrying that date against her ribs since a month four months back. She wanted it on that board.** **WHAT WENT IN: an hour worked.** From the fourth hour to the seventh, on a stretch that is on nobody's book, in her own hand with the flat of a second finger on a ticket in an oilcloth. **It is the ninth hole in a column and this one is exactly on time, and no book within nine days of road has heard the eighth.** **ON THE FAR SIDE, a keeper ruled a line, wrote the date at the left, did not fill the rest of it, and pushed the strip behind the plank — the same width as every other strip in nine years, including the ones nobody can read.** **THE MISTAKE, in four minutes, in the plainest words: you have put a hole on a board you cannot read.** The three empty dates in a wage-book are honest and they can be pointed at. **This one cannot.** She cannot take it back. A man with a boot tells her to her face that she is making another one in a thing he cannot see, and says he would like to have said it out loud once. **One stage short of finished, and a gap on a post that a man will keep for nine years more, and nothing she does before the twenty-ninth puts a line in it.** Loss. |
| 235 | What Merit Settles | Days 441–454, the 8th to the 21st. **BATCH CLIMAX** | **FOURTEEN DAYS.** A woman of eighteen at the eighth stage of a realm she has no paper for takes three-quarters of a mile past the cart turn to the end, alone, by hand, with a bar — **and the sub-contractor did not know she had started and there was nothing to stand.** Out at the fifth hour because the low side holds a weight wrong until the sun has been on it an hour; in by the seventh, because a body at four counts does not know how far it has come. A hold built about forty times an hour and about eleven lost a day. **ON THE FIFTEENTH SHE WENT IN** — the bar went down a foot and a bit instead of a foot and she had her weight over it, and she was in to about the hip before she had decided to be, and lay there for as long as it takes to count eleven, and the bottom came out and put her on her back in six inches of something that was not mud and was not water. **Nobody within a day and a half's walk. There is no version of this where she was rescued and there is no version where a man stood a stretch. She got up on her hands and did not go back into that spot for three days.** **THE TWENTY-FIRST: she laid the last mark** — no paint out past the cart turn, so she mixed the lime she has had since a month six months back with the water in the cut and laid it on with the flat of the bar end, a mark about the width of a hand, and a second at the foot of the rise, **the way a run is finished**, and wrote it on the ticket in one line of eleven minutes. **WHAT SETTLES, about a quarter of an hour later:** the job is on the list and it is finished and it has settled; **it is not a man, it never was a man**; and he is taking a share and spending it now out of her foundation and **it does not come back, and the base is one stage short of finished and that is where it comes out of, because that is the only place it is.** **He gave no figure and she did not ask.** The channel came back whole. **THE RIGHT INDEX FINGER, at about the sixth hour at the far end, nine feet off a mark laid in lime a quarter of an hour before.** It did not hurt; it went the way the order went out of her left hand and the way the heel went. **It is the finger that makes the turn at the end of a stroke, and the finger a licence nail goes in with, and there is one nailed straight above a bunk nine days west — she did not put it in, a voice she could not touch put it in, and the finger that did it is this one.** **Four fingers' worth of grip left on that hand, eleven holds in the other out of about seventy tried, and nothing in the world offers it back.** She picks the bar up off the ground with the other three fingers and the thumb and gets two feet up in one movement, which she can still do. **SHE TOLD THEM OUT LOUD at the plank, in the ordinary voice, before they could work out whether she was going to lose the other one.** A silence at the pot about as long as it takes to count eleven. **The sub-contractor wrote her down on the twenty-second and did not look up and said “Twenty-two. Five. Same as the rest,” and she did not thank him for it**, because thanking him would have made it a thing he had done for her. The man with the boot: **I said I would come on the eleventh and I did not come. / You did not come at all. / No. You did not.** She writes one line on the third ticket: *the finger is gone and it went for that.* Climax / cost. |
| 236 | What He Can Read | Days 455–461, the 22nd to the 28th | **SHE WALKS PAST THE CART TURN AND KEEPS GOING, for the first time** — along the whole of the far end and half a mile to a yard with stone in it and up onto low ground where the drift is six inches and does not ring. **THE BOARD IS THE LAST WORKING POINT ON NINE DAYS OF ROAD, and everything past it is somebody's ordinary country, and she does not go past it.** She can read the frame and not one word inside. She counts the lines, because counting is all she has ever been able to do with it — nine years, a strip every nine or ten days, **and in the middle of this month, between the thirtieth of the month before and about the ninth of this one, a strip with a date at the left and nothing at all on the rest of it, four inches wide.** The dated lines above and below it are readings, because **a reading is a number of yards worked at a place on a day by a man and she has been reading that kind of number for four years in nine counties and cannot be shown one by accident. The strip in the middle is hers and it is a hole the width of a stage.** **THE MAN WHO KEEPS IT, about fifty-five, NEW, gives her a knife and a spare strip because she is holding her right hand in a way that made the tool look like a thing to be done one-handed**, and he says *there is no one to tell that you are here* and *do not tell me what you are reading, because you cannot read it.* **He has not asked her one thing in two visits.** **THE HONEST PARTIAL ANSWER, once, against his own board:** the line is not stone and was never in the same place on both sides of itself and it does not move in a night; it is a line of work kept by people going out on it with bars in their hands; where they go and go on time it holds and where they do not it leaks; what comes out of a stretch that has not been kept is drift, **thick on the worst-worked ground and thin where nobody works, and his end of it is the thin end because there is nothing this side of the rise for it to come to.** **Ninety parts of that is what the men on your side of it say. I have been out nine years and I have never seen the other ten. If there is another ten parts of it then somebody will find them out by doing it and not by asking me.** **WHAT SHE DID WITH IT: she put it against four months of her own measuring and it did not move, said it to herself once in plain words, and built nothing on it and left the tenth part where she keeps the three empty dates.** **WHAT HE CAN READ, on the twenty-sixth, unasked, in one breath: “It says the Standing Office of the Second Account.”** Then: **that is a middle office, there are offices above that one and I have never seen one of them, and I am going to stop.** He stopped. **She wanted to ask whether it is the office that countersigned her petition more than anything else and did not, because a man who has said four times that he cannot read a thing and then reads it and then stops has told her everything he has.** **SIX WEEKS, on the twenty-eighth, unasked, four sentences: you have about six weeks of me and nothing in this country that makes it longer except a base that is not finished yet.** She writes it under the column of marks at eleven minutes a line. Twenty-one days to the ninth. Revelation. |
| 237 | What Goes Past The Line | Day 462, the 29th | **FOUNDATION 9, ANNOUNCED AT HER OWN ORDER, IN A PLACE WHERE NOTHING IS ANNOUNCED.** She takes it standing up at the fourth hour on the far end, with the bar in her left hand and two marks in lime still white enough to see from nine feet off. She says the word, does not hold it, does not shut her eyes, and lets it go. **[BRACKET] Foundation, stage nine, is held.** **WHAT IT DID HERE: NOTHING.** She looks at every person on nine days of working ground one after the other and not one face has changed — **there is nobody within nine days of road to be told, and about a quarter of a second after it goes into the ground she feels it go along, in one direction, east, past the post, out into country she has never been in.** She does not build a theory on it. **The base finishes while she is standing there and it is heavier and colder than eight ever was.** **WHAT IT DID A MILE PAST: a man of fifty-five comes up with the cart, takes a strip off against a rule, writes a date at the left, stops with the pen down about an inch above the cloth for as long as a man reads a line, then writes something she cannot read, pushes the strip behind the plank and goes down the rise.** **He did not come and ask her what the sound was. He wrote it down because that is what the board is for.** **EIGHT HUNDRED.** In a low room four hundred yards past the post a man at a counter is reading the conditions of a licence out loud to six men because that is what the counter does — fourth, sixth, and then the seventh: **a standing claim of eight hundred jin, entered on this licence, travelling with it, and not affected by it.** She is nine feet off, outside the door. **Nobody in that room knew she was standing there.** **WHAT IT MEANS FOR ONE PERSON, in short plain sentences:** the figure was read out, and that is all that has happened to it; and the people in that room can act on it, **and that is the part that is new** — the first office within nine days of road in a position to do anything about it, reached in the ordinary way on a piece of paper carried by a cart that was doing another job. **She did not go in**, because a woman with four fingers' worth of grip and no pot and no household and a licence she has never opened is not somebody that room has to act for, and because questions are not on the wage-book. **Four counties write what is heard. Nine counties hear it.** Anger and awe together, four minutes, and it did not need anything else. Reversal. |
| 238 | A Name In A Book | Days 463–470, the 30th and then the 1st to the 7th. **THE BREAK, AND THE LAST CHAPTER OF VOLUME 4** | **THE TWO WAYS THE BOUNDARY CAME DOWN, IN THIS ORDER.** **First the layer:** she goes past the marks on the thirtieth at about the fifth hour, half a mile from the post, and he says **“There is a line going into the board. It has a date on it and it is in the narrow column and it is the ninth thing in that column since before you came east. It does not stop. I cannot see you go.”** **And the first cost nothing at all. Then her body:** the high ground, then three-quarters of a mile of the low side at four counts with **four fingers on one hand and a bar in the other**, a day and a half, **one fall on the knee at about a third of the way along**, out at the fifth hour on the second. **Nobody found her and nobody was sent and there is not going to be a third.** **ON THE SECOND, at a trestle past the post: eleven men, a fire, a book open on a plank, and my name and I want the hours. He wrote it. That is the whole of what happened at that trestle and no man there asked her a single question.** It took **about eleven minutes** for the date, because the writing hand is the left one and it goes with the flat of the second finger, **and it took her longer than that because the other hand has four fingers and a thumb, and the man kept the book flat with his palm and the corner went twice, and she did not ask him to hold it the second time.** **THE PRICE, SAID OUT LOUD AT A TRESTLE IN THE ORDINARY VOICE BECAUSE THAT IS WHAT IS SAID ON A LINE:** “Five.” **He wrote the five in the price column, because that is what the price column is for, and did not look up.** **THE WARDEN'S HAND, on the fourth: he certifies that a person left the working ground on the thirtieth and came to the far side of it, dated on that side of it, and at the bottom where a person signs there is the office and not his name.** It is the second one he has signed; the first is in a county book nine days west and is the reason her course shut its ground, **and it is true and he would write it again, and it does not have his name on it and never has.** **WHAT WENT IN THE SPACE: the countersignature on her petition, in an office's hand, dated from the far side, because it is the only thing anybody above him could put there — and it is not an answer to his request and his request is still pending and there is still no instrument in his hand. The writ is issued by the people whose line it is, to somebody standing on it and carried by it; they closed their ground against her and they were right; they still do not know she exists; and there is no way of telling them and there is no writ.** He does not ask her to come. **THE FINAL IMAGE, element for element:** a wage-book open on a trestle, a column of hours that is hers, the price said out loud and written down without anybody looking up, and behind her **a board on a post, dated, nine lines in it, eight of them with something on them and one of them with nothing on it at all**, in a hand nobody on that line can read and that every person at that trestle can tell you is a hand from the other side — **and eleven men on that line have never heard an announcement at all in their lives. She has seen a column like that before and nobody else in that country ever has.** **Nine days of road behind her, and a floor, and nine men, and a tower with a light in it** — once. **And it was quiet: the first silence she had ever stood in that was not the inside of her own head.** Foundation nine, nothing above nine anywhere in the world, a finished base and the coldest thing about her, **about six weeks of a voice and a standing that is hers to order and she did not order it.** The register still reads reconciled; the plate is still against her ribs; the sheet went west with a foot and a half of blank paper in it and is never coming back; the claim is standing and not answered and the man who asked the question about it has not been given the thing he asked for and is not going to be. **The man at the trestle tore a strip off the book at the rule and put it on the plank face down and went and got a pot. There were six of those in an oilcloth against her ribs now, in three marks, and none of them could be checked against anything.** Decision / image. **Nothing follows it.** |

**Batch position at the end of ch 238 — VOLUME 4 CLOSED: Foundation 9. Day 470, the seventh of a month, and she is ACROSS.** On the far side of a line of work in a wage-book on a trestle with a column of hours that is hers. The right index finger gone, permanently, at the blind run. Nine Foundation stages, one suppressed, one heard past the line. Merit settled once, on a finished job. About six weeks of channel. Ledger taken twice in the volume and refused once, and hers to order at the last page and not ordered. The countersignature is in the space on the shelf and the request is still pending. The claim has moved to the first office that can act on it and is not answered. Nine lines on a post, eight written and one with nothing on it at all. MEMBER ABSENT three times and permanent, the sheet gone west for the last time this season. Five counterfoils became six, in three marks. The register still reads *reconciled*. The Clay Plate is still worn. She does not forgive him and she does not tell him to stop. **No figure of what was left of him is stated anywhere before ch 236 and none after it, nothing killed anything, and nothing was cleared except the one listed job.**

## Volume 4 closed — audited

**Audited against `outline/volume-04.md` line by line at the volume close. The full table — eighteen named
checks, the fifteen required continuity facts, the repair list, the findings recorded and not repaired, and
the three corrections to inaccurate state-file claims — is in `state/current.md` under
`## Volume 4 close audit — CLOSED`. Nothing moved: no title, no chapter order, no card beat, no bracket, no
ending, no pinned day.**

**What the close changed on the page, in four repairs, all word- or sentence-level:**

| Ch | Was | Now | Why |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **179** | *…because that is the finger that can still do a turn at the end of a stroke* | *…because a coin will go across the flat of that one and not across a fingertip* | The construction belongs to ch 235 and was being spent fifty-six chapters early |
| **220** | *it came on the nineteenth of this month* · *The stage came on the nineteenth* · *two days* ×2 | *it came on the seventeenth of this month* · *The stage came at the fourth hour* · *the rest of the day* · *the hours between the two of them* | Day 360 **is** the seventeenth, twenty-two days from the twenty-fifth of the month before, and the interval to day 386 is twenty-six only if the fifth stage is on the seventeenth. The chapter's own heading and nine later chapters date the strike to the seventeenth, so the stage was the error |
| **224** | *I would do the whole of it again on **Friday*** | *I would do the whole of it again on **any working morning*** | The volume's only named weekday, against a hard rule |
| **232** | *…and she had four fingers' worth of nothing on the other one that she had not noticed yet* | *…and every part of her that had gone wrong since the thaw had gone wrong on that side* | A direct pre-echo of the right index finger three chapters before ch 235 |

**Five findings recorded and not repaired, with the reason:** Bai Zhu's name is spoken twice more than the
outline's *named once* (ch 189, 196 — both authorised by this volume's own cards, and the operative rule that
no document he signs carries his name is intact) · the ch 234 cost is echoed at ch 236 and not at ch 235
(each cost is named exactly twice, never a third) · **Feng's fair hand copying the Foundation column and his
copies going past the line into a book the far side keeps are not on the page**, because he appears once in the
volume, at ch 181, and Volume 4's cards forbade a second appearance — **carried unspent into Volume 5** · the
far-side keeper's reason for giving her a knife at ch 236 · and the corrected month rule and word counts.

**The fifteen required continuity facts are confirmed one at a time, and three have changed shape: the office
above this county is NO LONGER UNREADABLE (read at ch 236 — the Standing Office of the Second Account, a
middle office, and everything above it still unread); Ledger is TAKEN TWICE IN VOLUME 4 AND REFUSED ONCE OUT
LOUD (eight days out of ten for a name, one day out of three for the crossing, hers to order and not
ordered); and the year of non-forgiveness CHANGED SHAPE AND IS STILL RUNNING — a purchase, not forgiveness,
and the account still carrying.**

**The protagonist question is answered in writing.** Yuan is the book's protagonist and he has been a voice
only, off-panel, for two volumes of sixteen. **Volume 5 takes the correction inside its own sixty chapters:
at least one sustained scene in which he acts, or is acted on, away from her hearing, and it takes something
that is his, because a man the reader has never seen in a room cannot carry the fall `outline/ending.md`
fixes for him.** Full statement in `state/current.md` and `workspace/volume-04-close/SUMMARY.md`.

**Volume 4 file set: 120,572 words over sixty chapters, chapter titles and section headings excluded. Eight
bracketed layer lines in the volume, one at each announced Foundation stage and none at all in ch 234. No
calendar month and no weekday is named anywhere in ch 1–238, and the weekday rule is now written into canon in
`bible/world.md` after the Volume 4 close review found it inferred, unenforced outside one volume, and broken
eight times in Volumes 1 and 2; all eight were repaired. Volume 5 is *Mortal Ash*, ch 239–298, and its outline
is written and its volume closed and audited. **Volume 6 is *The Real Realm*, ch 299–360; `outline/volume-06.md`
is written and `outline/batches/volume-06-batch-0001.md` is carded for ch 299–313.**

## Volumes 5–16

Titles and remaining chapter ranges in `outline/series.md`.
