- **Ch 305 — *The Strip Off The Cart* (days 631–632, the eighteenth and the nineteenth).** A strip of cloth off the top of the cart bag, the same cloth the keeper's board is made of, cut to the same shape, three ruled columns, nothing filled in them and three words in the line where a reach's totals go: **is there more.** It is about a reach, not a person, and the reach is hers. **The answer does not go back down into the counter's book — it goes up, or it does not go** — and a counter does not carry things up on his own initiative, because that is not what a counter is for and because it would put his room in it. She says *where did this come off*, gets four words, and leaves the strip lying. She stands in the field on the nineteenth and works out that a space in a book gets filled by whichever hand is holding the pen when the ordinary week comes round, and that what goes in it if she says nothing is not a woman who stopped. *Emotion, the first fear in five months that is about other people: it was easy for somebody to ask.*

- **Ch 306 — *Is There More* (days 633–634, the twentieth and the twenty-first).** The strip is in the book, face down, with a clean square of nothing beside it about the size of four fingers. She goes over three options in her own head in short sentences — say yes and be a running column, say no and be a finished person in a book for ever, say nothing and let the space be filled by a hand that has never seen her — and cannot take the third, because the space will be filled with a shape and then it will be true, and her days were the only thing she had ever had that belonged to her alone. The ring goes down and goes down further, because there is nothing to bank it with and a banked fire is not a fire. She opens a reach and a half on the twentieth and a reach and half again on the twenty-first: in no book, buying nothing, and the first work of her life done to be easier to bear. She sits on the boards and says *It is not holding*, which is about the clay. *Emotion: she is afraid of a room she has never stood in, and she is about to hand over the proof herself.*

- **Ch 307 — *The Cut She Went Down* (day 635, the twenty-second) — the batch's physical risk, unrescued.** A silted stone drain on the low side of Pei's field, up to the chest, cold, a man has to go in on his back, standing clay at the shoulder from rain two days back. **The bank comes in on her while she is on her back.** Four minutes in the dark under two feet of clay with a bar across her shoulder, thinking about the eighteen in four marks and a shelf with a sheet of paper on it and a door that stands open nine days west, and then not thinking about anything at all — **because a person in that position makes no sound whatever.** **There was nobody within a mile who would have come and not one of them would have come and there was no version of that afternoon in which a man stood in that cut.** She gets out with the bar in about as long as it takes to count eleven, alone, and nothing in her breaks that has not been had back once. She tells Pei the low side wants a spade and two men on a rope and does not tell him about the shoulder. *Emotion: anger at herself — the whole of four years of advantage spent underground, and it took a hole to find out.*

- **Ch 308 — *One Stair Up* (day 636, the twenty-third) — THE IRREVERSIBLE DECISION.** The counter cannot answer it and will not carry an answer up, so she goes round the end of the counter and up the stair. **She does not give her name; the room finds it** — read off a column and matched to the woman standing there. Four people copying, tables both sides, a stack of sheets as high as a man's chest, paste and warm paper. **The stair above it goes up out of the light and she looks at it for nine seconds and does not take it.** He asks the ordinary question about the ordinary gap — the twenty-second and the twenty-third to the fourth hour — and she confirms it, because the gap is true and hers, and says *I was under a bank*, and he writes it down. Then she says **there is more**, in the ordinary voice, in daylight, with nobody persuading her. *The price in one sentence about one person: her column was the only thing in the world she could prove about herself, and from the fourth hour of the twenty-third it is a thing somebody else can read.* She cannot be un-answered and the answer went up the stair with the answer.

- **Ch 309 — *Priced From Above* (day 637, the twenty-fourth) — CLIMAX, first half.** A strip on the gate post at eye height: **five coppers a reach, where it was seven on the fourteenth.** Pei reads it twice, folds it into his coat, gets the bar out and gives it to her, and is right — he is told, and a man with a field and a household does not decide for himself what an office above a copy floor thinks a woman is worth. The third reach on that low ground is the one she went under two days ago and she is not going into it again. **Six coppers.** The whole of it: *good* is a certificate, and a certificate says the person does not need the whole of a day's rate, and she wanted to be checked and she has been checked and it is cheaper than her and she is the one being sold at it. **She wanted to be checked. She has been checked. It is cheaper than her and she is the one being sold at it.** *The batch's anger — the first real one since the crossing — goes into the arithmetic and does not come out of her mouth: everything done to her in this country was done by somebody who was right, and being right is the part that costs her.* She does not thank him and is not going to.

- **Ch 310 — *Half A Reach* (day 638, the twenty-fifth) — GOLDEN CORE 4, the batch's only bracket.** Taken at her own order at the fourth hour in the middle of a reach on Pei's field, bar in her left hand, nobody within two hundred yards, **where nothing is announced** — no one inside to be told, no county book to take it; the sound goes into the ground and nothing comes back. *[Ning Yan, past the line, servant-born, under no ceiling any county holds. Golden Core, stage four, is held.]* Then the hand tells her: **the flat of a second finger from longer to longest, permanently**, so every line she writes anywhere now costs its hour twice over, and **the yard work she can still price one-handed narrows to half a reach.** **There was no part of her that was slower, and that is the part that is frightening.** Half a reach, **two and a half coppers**, taken. *Emotion: not the fear she had at the third — the flat recognition that the stage cost exactly what she had already spent, and one plain sentence: she went into that reach able to price a day's work with one hand and came out able to price half of one, and there was nobody in the world she could tell.*

- **Ch 311 — *The Brick Money* (days 639–641, the twenty-sixth to the twenty-eighth).** The material shed past the mill is a lean-to with about nine things standing on it, no counter, no prices, and a man who does not come out from behind the stack. **Nine coppers for a full charge — said once.** And the second fact that is the whole chapter: **a full charge does not come in a size one arm can carry**, and a bar does not cut brick, and the flat of a second finger is now the longest it has ever been. The arithmetic in four lines: half a reach a day at five is two and a half a day, three days is seven and a half, a charge is nine — **so four days of clearing for one charge, and then three trips for the same one.** There are three things on that ground that could move a sack half a mile — a clearing paid in something other than money, a cart round, a shed that owed her — and **she names all three out loud in the yard and takes none of them**, because the one thing on that ground that can pay for brick in a size one arm can carry is not coppers, and that is Batch 0002's to take. A third of a sack, carried back in two loads, and the ring lit low on the night of the twenty-eighth. *Emotion, the new thing: a want with no shape in it — she wanted a thing she could carry in one trip that would make the ring hold.*

- **Ch 312 — *The Bundle Going Up* (days 642–643, the twenty-ninth and the thirtieth) — Feng, by report, once.** A loaded cart going west stops at Pei's gate, and a man with a loaded cart stops for a question and not for nothing else. **One question, one answer, and she never asks a second:** *is there anything that comes back down that road* — *no. There is a stair about nine days west of here. Men go up it carrying bundles and they come down with nothing in their hands, and they have been doing that since before I was driving.* He gives her the rest for nothing, as a fact about a road: two days east past the flat ground and then more carts going up than down, and a loaded cart out of that yard every month whatever is in it and a driver who will take a person going that way. **She sits down on a board under a rope with a bar across her knees — the first time she has sat down in daylight since the seventh — and says nothing for half an hour.** In the mill yard she sees the stack for the first time: trays under sacking, on the ground, in the open air, roped twice. She does not go near it and does not count it and does not ask. On the thirtieth there is nothing on that ground that falls on a date, and she catches herself doing the old pay-day arithmetic and laughs once, out loud, alone. *Emotion: she wanted to tell somebody; she told a carter nothing, and he goes on driving and never thinks about that stair again, which is the correct ending for him and the worst one there is for her.*

- **Ch 313 — *The Ground Past The Mill* (days 644–645, the first and the second) — the last chapter of the batch.** She walks past the mill in daylight at the fourth hour on the first of a month with the bar over her shoulder, because there is no book to shut, no plank, no season and nothing that will close on anything. **Going past the wheel is not a thing.** Past the mill the ground goes flat, farmed and hedged, about four houses in four miles, a track that goes on east and does not come back. From two miles west a stack of trays is under boards and sacking in a yard and somewhere in it is her column with **fifteen gaps in it now instead of twelve**, a name at the top, and *good* above the hours; **the twenty-second is not in it**, because it is under two feet of clay on the low side of a field and she is the only living thing that knows. She cannot get to any of it. The three boards and the roof she built are at the yard and she is not going back. On the second morning she finds she is not afraid, and that noticing it is what takes her nine steps. *His last line of the batch, on the ground on the first night: it has been twenty-three days since you walked out of that yard, and that is the longest you have gone in one direction without stopping. The human sentence, plain, about one person: she was eighteen years old and standing in a country where an office could read her and a wage could be set for her and nobody within nine days of road knew her name, and she had been afraid of all three for five months and had gone on walking.*

- **Ch 314 — *Four Nails* (day 646, the third).** She turns back west to Pei's gate at the fifth hour, out at the seventh with half a reach and two and a half. **The arithmetic is dead in a new way:** the old count ran to one of three days; the new one runs to four days of gate work to a charge, and four days mean nothing except that the ring will be cold on the fifth. *The shape survived. The thing inside the shape did not.* At the fourth hour she finds a mortared wall on flat ground where there was nothing from two miles off — a stone-and-brick works, eight or nine men, **nine boxes in two rows and a tenth open with brick and clay broken into it in pieces about the size of two fists.** On the board: LISTED WORK. PAID IN WHAT THE WORK WAS MADE OF. NO HIRE. NO SEASON. NO NAME ON THE LINE. TAKE IT DOWN AND IT IS YOURS TO FINISH. Under it, one line: a low head and an apron at the lane crossing, four days, one box. **She takes the line down off the nails with the flat of a second finger, and it is the first document of any kind that has ever had nothing written on it anywhere and belonged to her.** **She asks when the cart goes** — and she has not thought about the box going anywhere, only about earning it. *The cart goes when the cart goes*, and the round is about three weeks. **One box is four days of the ring and worth about two coppers more than four days at a gate**, and she carried the board off the wall on the third and went west at the sixth hour. *Emotion: she had told herself there was nothing out here and had been wrong from two miles off, and she does not mind.*

- **Ch 315 — *The Lane Crossing* (days 647–650, the fourth to the seventh).** A head and an apron where a lane crosses a ditch, about nine feet by four, deeper on the far side because the far side is where the water comes out on. No hod, no plank — **a hundred and ten bricks under one arm up a bank of four feet, one at a time.** On the second day her right hand goes open on a brick and it goes off the bank into the ditch, and it lands on stone and stays there, and the far side of that ditch is the ground that is gone. She goes down for it with a rope off the hedge and gets nothing off the wall. She finishes in **three** days where the board said four and puts the line back up with the three days written on the back in her own hand. **The man at the gate will not give her the day she did not use: *The day you did not use goes back on the board. I cannot give it to you and I am not going to try.*** And it is the fourth time in five months that a man on that ground has been right about something, and **for the first time in five months being right has cost her nothing at all.** The box is counted out for her over the afternoon — **a man had broken it to a carrying size for her, and she has broken every piece of clay she has ever bought herself, one lump at a time, on a flat stone at the dark, about eleven minutes a lump.** At the fourth hour of the seventh, with nothing on her at all, **the cold comes up through the base — and her right hand is warm, and it is not the brick.** *Emotion: focus, and the first cold thing the ground has ever sent up to her.*

- **Ch 316 — *It Came Twice* (days 651–654, the eighth to the eleventh).** The cold comes up twice more and it is not fear — she has been under a bank and she knows exactly where fear sits in her. Under the gate post he tells her, before he spends it, that it comes off a listed thing when the listed thing is finished and that he wants a line. **One line, in a record an office reads: that the work at the lane crossing was cleared by the person whose days are in the book on the floor above a counter. No figure, no rate, no date. It goes up and it cannot be un-put.** She says *take it*, out loud, on the flat, in the ordinary voice, which is the arrangement and has been for four years. **The price, in one sentence: from the fourth hour of the tenth of a month there is no work of hers in the world that nobody can read.** Her column had a thirtieth in it and then it shut. This does not have a thirtieth. She goes to the yard, reads three new lines, **takes none of them for the first time in her life**, and says out loud to three lines of somebody else's handwriting that you do not get to choose what it takes, you get to choose whether you take it. **Then she goes west and the ring is out. Not banked. Out.** *Emotion: dread, and the flat recognition that she has traded the last thing that was hers alone for a line she cannot take back.*

- **Ch 317 — *The Ring Went Out* (days 655–657, the twelfth to the fourteenth).** A third of a sack is three coppers and she has two and a half. **The ring is not nine. Nine is a full charge and she has bought one in five months. Three coppers every fourth day is the ring, and it goes out on the fifth day anyway** — hot for about a day and a half out of every four, and she has never once written that down anywhere because there is nothing to write it in. Two days of half a reach and a cold corner and a night on flat ground in a coat. **What she feels is not anger — the anger was at a gate on the twenty-fourth of the last month and there was nobody there to spend it on. What she feels is that she has been *spent*.** She stands in a gateway with nine dressed pieces on the ground and works the whole arrangement out in four sentences: she clears a listed thing, a listed thing settles something, the fire and the settling are the same ground, and **the gate is not listed and a gate does not settle anything at all.** She carries a third of a sack to the yard and leaves it in a corner under the lean-to, and she works out that **she now owns a thing any person on that ground may use and that they will use correctly.** Then she takes the second line off the board: foot of retaining wall gone under the ditch between the two farms, four days, two boxes. *Emotion: spent, and the want with a shape in it — the ring lit tonight.*

- **Ch 318 — *Six Miles To The Fire* (days 658–661, the fifteenth to the eighteenth).** Nine feet of old footing out, about four feet of new one in, and the course at about a foot down where it had stopped being stone and started being clay. On the fifteenth she buys a third of a sack for three coppers, carries it three miles each way in two loads, breaks it at about nine minutes past the ninth hour — eleven minutes a lump — and banks the ring. **Six miles and eleven minutes a lump and three coppers to get one fire.** She works out what she has actually been buying since the eighth of the last month: **not heat. Answer.** *A woman who has a thing that answers her cannot be anywhere without it* — and a thing that answers is a thing she can be found at, which she has known since the sixteenth and has not wanted to look at. **Then the rule she works out, on the seventeenth and the eighteenth, in the ordinary voice at a ditch: two and a half a day, five coppers a reach, half a reach is what her body can open, and the reach goes down every time she takes something. The gate pays by the reach. The board pays by the job.** *For four years the only price on anything has been what I can lift.* **A box is four days of the ring and a settlement takes the fire that is in the ring that day, so a listing pays for itself in fire and the rest of the box is the money.** She goes down into the ditch at the fourth hour on the nineteenth.

- **Ch 319 — *The Block In The Ditch* (days 662–665, the nineteenth to the twenty-second).** Four days on twenty feet of retaining wall footing that has gone under a ditch. On the second day she gets a dressed block up off the clay, turned on its edge, and **her right hand opens — not the arm, the hand — and the block comes about a foot and a half and meets the bar**, and the sound goes out across the flat ground about as far as the mill. **There were four men on that ground within two hundred yards and every one of them stopped, and not one of them made a sound.** One goes back to the foot and starts taking clay out on the other side of the ditch without hurrying about it. She cannot get the block out of the ditch with a rope and a bar and half an hour. She finishes the course anyway, at about the seventh hour of the fourth day, with a wedge she made out of a piece of the broken block. **The man at the gate: twenty-three, not twenty-five. You are owed twenty-three and not twenty-five. The block is the yard's and not yours, because you are not hired here.** **She wanted to owe something.** *Emotion: loss, and a want she cannot name — she wanted to be a woman with two blocks of a wall of her own that a yard could come and take off.*

- **Ch 320 — *A Thing That Is Not Hers* (days 666–672, the twenty-third to the twenty-seventh).** Twenty-three counted into a box, and the box stands against the wall with no mark on it among nine that have one, and the cart does not go for about three weeks. She sits in a field at about the second hour and works out the whole of it: she can carry a box in eight loads of three miles on days when she is not working, and there is not one such day. **Not because of the arm. Because the yard's rule is that material leaves the yard against a name in a book.** The cold comes up on the twenty-fourth, twice, and **he tells her before he spends it: he wants the reach to put a thing on that ground that is not hers and not his and does not stop because of either of us.** **It is the first thing he has ever asked her for that she had not had to think about whether she wanted to give.** She says *do it*, and she sits for about nine minutes looking for the cost and there is none, and the thing that stops coming up afterwards is **the cold that tells her anything has happened at all.** On the twenty-eighth she goes to the mill, breaks four lumps, lights the ring — and waits. **It is warm. That is all it is.** *Emotion: grief with nothing to spend it on, and a thing she bought with a week of her own wall that only does what it is told.*

- **Ch 321 — *Warm Hands* (days 673–674, the thirtieth and the first).** **Eight days** is how long twenty-three lasts a ring that answers nothing — two boxes by the yard's figure of twenty-five, less the block. She goes west on the last night of the month and sleeps on the boards, and at the fourth hour of the first of a month she stands in the mill yard with the ring out on the flat stone by the door, lit low, and the shed door open behind her, and the **stack of trays against the far wall under boards and sacking, roped twice, about as high as a man** — hers is in one of them and she does not know which and is not going to look. The stage comes up standing, with the bar laid down on the stone. **There is no announcement and there is not going to be one: nobody inside to be told, no county book on that ground, and the sound of a stage is a sound and a sound has to go somewhere.** *[Ning Yan, past the line, servant-born, under no ceiling any county holds. Golden Core, stage five, is held.]* It went into the ground under that yard and into the air a quarter of a mile and a dog on a farm a mile off did not bark. **She puts her palm flat on the stone and the stone is cold. She takes her hand off and it is not cold.** *Emotion: awe, and the first warmth she has ever had that she did not make.*

- **Ch 322 — *What Her Hand Could Not Say* (days 675–678, the second to the fifth).** Five days and it has not improved and she is not expecting it to. **The cold has not gone out of her the way a thing goes out of a room — the world is still cold and the wind still comes in under the shed door and goes through her coat. It has gone out of her hands.** The hand has been her instrument for four years — a stone floor, a lump of clay, a bar at the top of a drain at the second hour — and on the third she puts her palm flat on a dressed block that has been out all night and it tells her the temperature of her own hand. Frost comes off stone between the second and the fourth hour and makes the top of it slick. **She puts her hand on nine stones on the morning of the fourth and gets nine of the same answer and goes on working.** On the fourth the block comes off the stone and goes across her thigh and the bar catches it at the edge of her knee and holds it a quarter of a second longer than she needed. She lies on the yard stone about four minutes and checks the four things in the order she always checks them. **A woman with four fingers and no cold in her hand is going to pick up a stone one day that she should not have picked up, and there is nobody within nine days of road who is going to be there when she does.** On the night she lies on flat ground until the second hour trying to find out whether the night is going to hurt her and the instrument is warm. *Emotion: fear, the first real one in a month, and a want with no plan attached — she wants to be able to put her hand on a stone in the dark and be told something.*

- **Ch 323 — *The Thing That Answers* (days 679–681, the sixth to the eighth).** Nine pieces out of the box in an hour. Eleven on the seventh, and a chipped corner left where it lay. On the eighth she gets four hundred yards and has to put it down and go back, and the one in her hand is the one she cannot feel. **It is for the ring.** A third of a sack is three coppers and about a day's ring, bought every fourth day; twenty-three pieces out of two boxes is about eight days of the ring; eight days of the ring is twenty-four coppers; twenty-four coppers is ten days at a gate. **Nine pieces in an hour is about two fifths of the box, and the box is eight loads and a little better than two days a load — sixteen days of it, against three weeks of waiting.** **She cannot take it out of the yard.** At the yard gate on the eighth, out loud, in the ordinary voice, to nine pieces of stone: *The material is for the thing that answers.* The answering went out of her on purpose on the twenty-fourth of the last month, by her, for a reach, and it did not come back when she lit it again, and **there is no material in the world that will put it back.** *Emotion: flat resolve, and the closed loop — nine pieces on a stone, a box against a wall, and one thing on that ground that can move it and it is not her.*

- **Ch 324 — *The Man Who Keeps The List* (days 682–685, the ninth to the twelfth).** The man who keeps the board is not the man behind the stack. Ink on the side of his right hand, and he knows she has taken two off that board without looking at the box. She asks, once, in daylight, in the ordinary voice, at the gateway, what it costs to have twenty-three carried three miles. **He cannot make the cart go and he cannot stop her carrying it.** It stops at his gate because a yard that lets material out on two arms cannot tell anybody whose a load was if it does not arrive — **so the yard keeps an account, and there is no account on that ground with her name on it.** She has one in a book about her days; **that is a different book and it is not his.** He tells her, once, and he does not enjoy it, that **there is an office that keeps the list this board comes from**, and that the lines are set somewhere else and come down and he puts them up, and that the office keeps a book of what the ground owes and what has been paid on it, and everything that goes out of the yard against a name goes into that book and does not stop at that yard. **The cost is that it is copied, and nobody has to do anything to her for that to happen.** Then the third thing: **the yard's board is a board, and the office has a list of its own that comes down about four times a year and is not put up where a woman can take it off. It is offered.** **She has known the yard for six days and has been doing listed work off a board for a week.** On the ninth she asked *whether* a woman not on the books was allowed to ask; an offer is made to a person. She is going to have to decide it in daylight in front of somebody. *Emotion: the pleasure of an institution explaining itself exactly, and the dread on the other side of it.*

- **Ch 325 — *The Cart Turned Round* (days 686–688, the thirteenth to the fifteenth).** The cart is against the wall with its shafts down and it is not going anywhere that day, which is a fact about that yard on that morning in the way the wheel of a mill is a fact about that yard every morning of the year. At the ninth hour the man behind the stack lifts the shafts, turns the cart about nine inches, and calls the man who keeps the list — **and he writes a line with nothing at the other end of it, and does not stop halfway through, and does not ask her anything.** *That is out, and there is nothing against it. That is a hole. I do not have a name for it and I have had this board eleven years and it has not had one.* Twenty-three pieces go on, roped twice, in about nine minutes, and the cart turns left on the made road and goes west to the mill. She rides the near shaft. **On the road she works out where it came from, alone, in about nine minutes: a round is a line, and her column is a line, and a hand in her line put his hand in a line about a cart — and he had told her what it was for nineteen days earlier on the twenty-fourth of the last month, before, out loud, at a ditch, and she had had nineteen days to stop him and had not known it was coming.** The listing man is standing at his bench looking at the line when the cart goes out. **A hole in a book with a man's name at the end of it and nothing at the other end, and eleven days to find out about it, and a man with a yard is going to have to answer for it.** *Emotion: shock — she did not ask, she did not promise, she did not buy it, and a thing came apart in a country she has been in five months on the strength of a week of her own wall.*

- **Ch 326 — *A Hand In The Book* (days 689–691, the sixteenth to the eighteenth).** Twenty-three pieces broken to a carrying size on the flat stone by the door, in the corner of the shed behind a third of a sack that went out on the seventh of the last month — **and she does not light the ring.** She worked this out on the eighth at a gateway standing up in daylight: the material is for the thing that answers, and the answering went out of her on purpose. On the eighteenth she stops at the elms and works out what happened to him. **A round is a line. Her column is a line about her. A hand in her line put his hand in a line about a cart — and it did not have to be about her at all.** He had been inside a line since the seventeenth of a month five months back and findable since, and on the thirteenth he had gone from a line about a woman to a line about a cart in one move and it had taken him about nine seconds. **There is no place on that ground where he can do that and not be in it.** The turn she turns over twice on the road: **she spent a week of her own wall on the twenty-fourth to buy a reach so that a box would go three miles, and a box that cost a week of her wall moved a cart.** He says it in the shed with the clay cold: *there is a line for the yard now, and I am in it.* What she is actually afraid of is not him. **It is that a line for the yard is a line, and lines go up, and a woman two miles and four hundred yards west has a column with her name at the top of it and a man nine miles east has put a blank on a board because of her, and there is no way to be a person that a blank can be traced to — and in about eight days she is going to stand in daylight at a gate and say her name out loud and ask to be put into another one.** *Emotion: cold recognition, and no forgiveness and no instruction.*

- **Ch 327 — *The Fire She Did Not Need* (days 692–695, the nineteenth to the twenty-second).** She lights it full on the nineteenth at the ninth hour — the first whole heap in five months — and it is the best fire within nine days of road, and the shed is warm for about nine feet, **and her hands do not tell her anything about it.** *The heat is as far as a hand goes, and she had known that since the eighth of the last month, and she had gone on paying for it anyway.* **The material is for the thing that answers, and the thing that answers went out of her on the twenty-fourth of the last month, and there is no material in the world that puts it back.** She waits about nine minutes with her hand on the brick at the eleventh hour and nothing comes, and she does not go on trying. **What a clearing paid in materials bought her: eight days of a fire in a shed three miles east of a yard, and a woman sitting on the boards beside it at the eleventh hour of the twentieth, eighteen years old, with the best fire within nine days of road.** What the clearing asked of a body: **seven days — three on a head and an apron and four on a footing** — a hundred and ten bricks under one arm, twenty feet of footing, a block in a ditch, twenty-three pieces at eleven minutes a piece, and **the reach — the part of her a clearing settles out of, which took the fire the first time and the answering the second, both of which she was told the price of and agreed to in one word.** And half a reach at Pei's gate on the twentieth and the twenty-first, **because two and a half a day is bread and bread does not settle anything, and the gate has never once in five months not been exactly her and will be exactly her again in about nine months.** *Emotion: the valley. She bought the one thing on nine days of road she no longer needed, at the most expensive rate anything has ever been bought on that ground.*

- **Ch 328 — *Three Days On It* (days 696–699, the twenty-third to the twenty-sixth; day 699 is shared with ch 329, which opens in the same gateway).** Three days to do it in, and nothing on that ground knows there is anything to do. On the twenty-fourth the round comes at the fourth hour and **the listing man writes the line off the thirteenth out again with the day on it and nothing at the other end, and rules a line across under it and starts on the next one**, and he tells her what he is going to do about it: charge it to the yard, not to her — the yard is short two boxes of stone and forty miles of hauling and that goes in on the yard's side, signed by a man whose name is on the end of the board. **And then the other half of it, which is the batch's worst thing at the hands of a right man: it is a hole, it is the first in eleven years, and a man with a yard is going to want to know whether his board can be taken out from under him, and the ordinary thing that man will do is take the cart off his own round until somebody puts something on the line.** *I am not saying that to put it on you. I am saying it because you asked me and I would rather you had it all.* She says *I am sorry about the cart* and *do not lose it on account of me*, and there is no arrangement between them that can be finished with the words. Two nights of weighing on flat ground: what she gets — a price for work that does not fall when her body gets stronger, a listing that comes to a person instead of a wall, and material that will still be hers on ground nine miles east of a yard — and what it costs: **a second office, a hand she has never seen, a book that is copied, material copied further than days are because days stay on the ground they happened on, and an entry that cannot be taken back by her, by leaving, or by walking a season's road the other way.** *If she says yes there will be two books on nine days of road with a person in them that is the same person, and she will never see either of them, and from that hour there will not be one hour of the rest of her life that she gets to keep.* **She has been in that yard about three weeks and everybody in it has been right every single time.** On the twenty-sixth she stops nine feet short of the bench and says her name out loud, in daylight, at a gate. *Emotion: the flat calm of a decision, and nobody who argued her either way.*

- **Ch 329 — *She Asked At The Gate* (day 699, the twenty-sixth; the twenty-seventh is her closing walk west and is not dated on the page) — THE IRREVERSIBLE DECISION AND THE VOLUME'S MIDPOINT.** *Ning Yan. Of Lowwater. I am asking to be put in the materials account of the office that sets this board.* He does not ask her to say it again. **He makes her say the last part again, and she does, and he tells her that most of them say it as a wage and have to be taken back and made into a materials, and that it takes four days longer. It is not a hiring: no day-book, no rate, no day, no stub, nothing torn off anything. If the office lists a thing and she clears it, what the thing was made of comes to her account and she takes it where she are standing.** He writes it in ink at the eighth hour on a strip about as wide as a hand with a ruled line and a line across near the bottom, cut at the bottom edge with a knife, and turns it face down on the bench because that is what a man does with a thing that is going somewhere and is not his. **The word, said out loud once, in daylight: it is a vouching. It is the same act twice and it is not the same thing twice. That one was about your days. This one is about material.** The price, in four short sentences with nothing added: *you cannot take it back — not by walking a season's road the other way, not by leaving, not by telling a man at a gate that you did not mean it — it is in a book and the book is copied and the copies go to a floor she has never been in in a hand she has never seen and nobody in nine days of road can take her out of it.* She is asked to be sure, because after this there is not one hour she gets to keep, and she has been sure for three days. **She said yes and it was not a fight and nobody was in the room except a man doing the thing he was built to do.** What it closes, standing up in about a minute: a yard that had been giving her work with nothing on any board about who she was; one price on her; a cart a man was going to lose — and a second book she will never see, next to the first, neither seeing the other. **Leaving the reach no longer takes her out of the books.** *Emotion: a plain, ordinary pleasure that is the first thing in five months she has asked for — and it cost her the rest of her life, out loud, in the light, at a gate, with her name on it.*

### Batch 0003 (ch 330–345) — sending and being sent for. WRITTEN AND REPAIRED, 25,056 words over sixteen chapters, average ~1,566, day 701 to day 735 walked plus the pinned day 749 for GC7 and the dated days 750–755, 763–764 and 773 that follow it, TWO bracketed layer lines, at ch 332 and ch 342.

**Reviewed and repaired at `logs/volume-06-batch-0003.review.log`: eleven manuscript defects, seven minor prose defects, and one false state premise corrected across seven files. No beat, title, turn, midpoint, climax, irreversible decision, bracket or stage moved; no chapter before ch 330 was opened. Full record in `state/continuity.md` under *Volume 6 Batch 0003 — REVIEW REPAIR*.**

- **Ch 330 — *The Name Against Material* (days 701–702, the 28th and 29th).** The board in the works' gateway has
  gained a ruled space about two fingers wide at the left, because since the twenty-sixth a line cannot leave the
  yard against nothing. **A materials account is paid to a place, not to a person**, and she gives the mill — the
  only building in nine days of road with a fire of her own in it — in about the time it takes a man to write four
  words. He tells her, unprompted, that the mill has a book that shuts on the thirtieth and goes up the mill road to
  the counting house. On the twenty-ninth she sees six lines on the board and her name at the left of one of them
  and five empty spaces the width of two fingers where the next five women will be. She takes the second line off
  the wall, a head at the low drain, two days and one box. *Emotion: the plain, unpleasant fulfilment of a month of
  wanting — she wanted to be findable and findability has a size, and the size is one line out of six.*

- **Ch 331 — *The Column In Two Hands* (days 703–704, the 30th and the 1st).** The copying floor. The man with the
  brush: **every month he copies her column out against another book, by hand, because the two of them were put
  across from each other on the twenty-third of a month back.** She asks what a cross-reference is for and is told,
  once, in the ordinary voice: *so that a thing about you is also a thing about what you have done.* She watches a
  woman of forty copy one column for half an afternoon at four words to a line and understands that being read is not
  being watched — **it is being the eight hundred strokes of somebody's ordinary afternoon.** He says the floor has
  been his father's, that a line out west is kept by men paid by the day with families on it, and that nobody east
  of here has ever needed one thing out of it. *Emotion: dread, and the discovery that findable was only half of it.*

- **Ch 332 — *Eight Holds* (days 705–712, the 2nd to the 9th; the stage on the ninth).** **GOLDEN CORE 6**, taken
  standing in the open out past the last hedge, bar laid on the grass, nothing within half a mile.
  *[Ning Yan, past the line, servant-born, under no ceiling any county holds. Golden Core, stage six, is held.]*
  **Cost, named once, permanent: nine holds to eight**, told before spent at the sill of a ditch on the seventh and
  spent into a head at the low drain she cleared by hand in two days. A hold is not a finger and it is not the arm:
  **it is the difference between a hand closing on a thing and a hand closing on nothing while the weight goes
  somewhere else.** The nine counting days she builds to find out what it cost are the chapter's last beat. *Emotion:
  the first night in five months she has gone to a thing she cannot feel.*

- **Ch 333 — *The Clearing She Bought* (day 712, the ninth — the same day as Golden Core 6, corrected at review).** The low drain, finished, one box. Nothing
  comes up through the base and she is not disappointed about it — **she has not been able to use that instrument
  since the twenty-fourth of the last month and she cannot tell whether the hold is gone until she checks the four
  things in the order she always checks them.** The gate and the yard do not move: eight holds bought her a
  clearing she could not have cleared before, and the board does not go down. **There were six lines on that board
  and eight holds in her hand, and the two are not a ratio.** *Emotion: the dread of a world that is exactly the
  size it was for the last woman who stood in it.*

- **Ch 334 — *The Cart Comes Back* (days 715–717, the 12th to 14th).** The round, the eleventh hour. Eleven boxes
  go on the cart with her name at the left of the line and the mill under it, and the cart the listing man said he
  was going to lose comes back into his own round **because of her.** He tells her so in the ordinary voice and adds
  that nobody had ever said so to him and he is not going to make a thing of it. She walks two and a half miles on
  the near shaft of a cart going into a book that shuts on the thirtieth, and works out in the road that she wanted
  her name on a wall **and there is no version of a name on a wall that does not also go into a book.** She does not
  look at her own page when the tray-man comes out of the shed. *Emotion: guilt she cannot spend.*

- **Ch 335 — *He Does Not Want It Answered* (days 718–719, the 15th and 16th).** **The Hunter, on the page.**
  Bai Zhu at nine feet off a cart track, notebook shut, buys nothing, asks nothing, attends and certifies and writes
  four lines a day. He has seen the shape of the new paper twice and will not say what is on it: *a column and a
  holding under it. The last shape was a line and a line is a thing that gets found. This has a holding under it.*
  And then, once, in the ordinary voice, **I do not want it answered either** — an answered thing goes up and
  somebody above him has to reckon with the answer, and he has never believed the reckoning would be the right one.
  The line in short sentences: kept by men, paid by the day, families on it, nineteen years, and **he cannot tell
  her why it is there and has met four men who worked it and not one could tell him.** His paper is still with the
  district; a writ is not issued to a field agent. *Emotion: the relief of a man who wants nothing, and the first
  real information in a month.*

- **Ch 336 — *A Strip With Two Columns* (days 720–722, the 17th to 19th).** **THE SENDING IS WRITTEN.** A strip about
  as wide as a hand comes off the bundle at the works. Two columns: five months of her days in the counter's hand,
  and under them **two ruled lines with nothing at the other end of the rule** — a materials account against the
  mill, and the holder, in a fine hand. The right column: a mark that is not a name, four figures, the word HELD, a
  rule, and two words under it she cannot read. Four lines at the foot: *what is copied at this reach is copied
  twice; the column of Ning Yan, of Lowwater, is kept and is copied and is not on this ground; the holder is to be
  presented in person in the room named under the holder; the doors of that room shut on the twenty-eighth of a
  month.* **The room is on the copy, and the copy is above the counter, and she does not go and find out.**
  *Emotion: the cold of being told what a person is in a column for.*

- **Ch 337 — *What The Paper Asks* (days 723–725, the 20th to 22nd).** She asks what presented in person means and is
  told: *a person goes into a room and does not come out of it again. Everything else that comes off that road is a
  paper.* Two of about four hundred strips in eleven years have had two columns. The listing man gives the line in
  four short sentences — **a road west about nine miles, men on it, paid by the day, I had an uncle on it, nobody
  here has ever needed one thing out of it** — and then: *and now there is.* At the second hour she gives the price
  before the word, and he takes about a minute and says it once: **it goes when I am held, and he will not be
  nowhere afterwards; what she gets is the fire, holding without her.** She says *all right* — not yes, because she
  has said yes to two other men in a month — and tells him to say when it has gone, and she cannot tell and knows it.
  *Emotion: dread and a want with a shape in it — she wants the fire too, and that is the horror.*

- **Ch 338 — *The Room With Doors* (days 726–728, the 23rd to 25th) — YUAN ADDRESSED AS A HOLDER. THE HAND SPENT.**
  **The sustained scene in which he is on the page in his own right, in his own frame, away from her hearing. Six
  utterances, every sentence present tense. Not a re-run of ch 272.** A room, not a field: a floor, a table, a chair
  pushed back at an angle, two shut doors and one with a handle. A hand with a brush writes slowly and is making a
  thing that will afterwards be correct, and it writes **HELD** under her days. **A line is a thing that gets
  found. A holding is a thing that can be asked for.** A holding has to be held by name, and a name is a word an
  office can put on a strip — so holding it costs the last thing in him that no office can write, **and he will not
  be nowhere afterwards.** He reaches for the morning he died; it is not there; he does not look again. He says the
  price back before he does it, because he always does. **The spend comes out of her foundation** — not her hands,
  the thing under her base — and a ring in a shed at a mill comes up full with nobody in the building. He speaks
  last in the room: *you do not get to have me twice, and you had the other one free, and you gave it away for a fire
  in a shed, and I am not sorry.* *Emotion: grief plain and unadorned, and a kind of shame he names in the room.*

- **Ch 339 — *The Fire Without Her* (days 729–731, the 26th to 28th).** She does not get up at the fourth hour. At
  the second hour the shed is warm to about nine feet and the ring is up against the corner at full, banked to a
  third on the nineteenth and lit by nobody. Her hand cannot tell her, so she uses the back of her wrist, the way a
  person does when a hand has stopped working. **She tests it properly — two days, three miles off, the fire up
  both nights — and then sits on the boards and puts the whole of it in one line, because a thing she does not say
  out loud comes out of her sideways later.** She does not forgive him and she does not tell him to stop, and neither
  of those is about him. *Emotion: a gift she did not ask for and cannot spend.*

- **Ch 340 — *The Book On The Thirtieth* (days 732–733, the 29th and 30th).** The tray on the bench and the cord going
  round it; **four trays since she walked out past the mill and one every month for as long as she clears a listed
  thing** (four, corrected at review — the chapter enumerated three and then corded a fourth). She asks where her material goes and is told the truth in four sentences: *a day ends and a material does
  not; this is the book that shuts and that one has been open since before I came and will be open when I am gone;
  copies of yours go in this one, by hand, at the end of every month.* **The book is open on the counter with six
  men at their cups and she does not look at her own page**, which costs her something and is the right cost.
  *Emotion: the plain human sentence — a line is not much, and it is the only thing she ever got about her brother.*

- **Ch 341 — *Four Counts And A Load* (days 734–735, then fourteen days carried as elapsed).** A head at the second
  gully, **one day, one box**, which she loads in three instead of nine because a day is a day. **Four pieces under
  one arm up a bank of four feet with the bar in her left hand, and her breath goes to five on the second load at
  the third count** — and comes back to four when she puts them down. She goes up eleven times to find the edge.
  Then fourteen days, carried as elapsed and not walked: eleven worked, one unwatched at the mill breaking up a sack
  for nothing, five mornings counting on a loaded reach, four every time. *Emotion: the flat preparation of a woman
  who already knows and wants to be certain.*

- **Ch 342 — *Five Counts* (day 749, the sixteenth — pinned).** **GOLDEN CORE 7**, taken standing in the open on the
  same ground. *[Ning Yan, past the line, servant-born, under no ceiling any county holds. Golden Core, stage seven,
  is held.]* **Cost, named once, permanent: the short breath goes past four counts where work is heaviest — five
  counts on a loaded reach — and never comes back to four there.** Four everywhere else, always. Eleven climbs in an
  hour and the place where it goes to five does not move. And the same evening, at Pei's gate: **half a reach was
  two and a half; a third of a reach is one and a half**, taken out of the fold in the inside of the door frame.
  *Emotion: the flat recognition that the stage cost exactly what it always costs, and there is still nobody in the
  world she can tell.*

- **Ch 343 — *Five And Not Four* (days 750–755, the 17th to the 22nd — corrected at review; the first record said undated).** The gate arithmetic, done on a gate post. **One and a
  half a day is not bread**: it buys bread and about a third of what was left over on two days in seven, and a third
  of a sack of clay is three coppers — so the fire she was given for nothing has turned an exactly balanced
  arrangement into one that does not pay. **The day she lost:** the last eighteen inches of the low side of a
  four-foot cut, undercut a foot and a half, goes under her right foot with sixty pounds on her arm; four feet down
  on wet clay for about eleven minutes, nobody within nine days of road, and she comes out with the load and loses
  the whole day. **Five days where the board said four**, and the listing man is right and says so and does not say
  it at length. On the walk back she wants the board to put up a line her hand cannot do, and there will not be one.
  *Emotion: arithmetic, and the first angry sentence she has said out loud since the gate.*

- **Ch 344 — *The Board Is For That* (days 763–764, the 30th and then the 1st of the month after it — corrected at review; the first record said undated) — THE BOX CIRCUIT IS BROKEN.** The room with
  the counter, the second hour, the book open with a stone on it. **She reads her own column for the first time** —
  five months and four gaps, one of them four fingers wide where a bank came in on her under a field. And at the
  foot of it, ruled under the thirtieth, **two lines with nothing at the other end of the rule**: the materials
  account against the mill, and the holding in the fine hand. A book that shuts has a beginning and an end on
  everything in it. **The man with the brush cannot take them out, cannot rule across them, writes the day in the
  margin against both, and goes up the stair to ask.** Four hours later a strip comes down with four words on it:
  **It goes as it is.** The tray is corded with a page fanned open in it and goes up the mill road at the sixth
  hour. A strip comes off it. **The cart stops at the post and a keeper of about fifty-five who has kept that board
  nine years reads it, works the paste round the pot with his boot, and presses it down with the flat of his hand —
  because that is what the board is for.** *Emotion: none. Nothing in the chapter is a decision and the chapter
  knows it.*

- **Ch 345 — *It Does Not Close Clean* (day 773, the tenth, ten days after the cart that carried the strip down on the first — corrected at review; the first record said undated) — the batch's last chapter.** The strip
  on the board **is not lying flat**: it has a crease a third of the way down instead of in the middle and it stands
  out past the edge of the board by the width of a finger, and the keeper explains, correctly, that a strip is
  folded in two in the middle when the thing it is a strip of has been shut. **From that hour nothing goes up that
  road shut, and she broke it by working** — four listed things cleared in seven weeks and a name asked for at a
  bench in daylight. The rule that has run for nine years is broken too: **the first thing in nine years has come
  back down that road, and it is a question and not a reading.** She can read her own name on it. She cannot read the
  fine hand. *Emotion: not fear — a want with a shape in it, and it is the only thing she has left to want.*


### Batch 0004 (ch 346–360) — standing and refusing to stand. WRITTEN, REVIEWED AND REPAIRED, 21,479 words over fifteen chapters, average ~1,432, day 774 to day 848, TWO bracketed layer lines, at ch 352 and ch 357. **THE LAST BATCH OF VOLUME 6.**

**Drafted against `outline/volume-06.md` and against `workspace/volume-06-batch-0004/PROMPT.md`, which is this batch's card. Every pinned item in it is on the page: Golden Core 8 at ch 352 on the pinned day 786, Golden Core 9 at ch 357 on the pinned day 818, the Hand refused once out loud at ch 355, the sending refused at ch 358, what breaking cost at ch 359, and Nascent Soul 1 at ch 360 on the pinned day 845 with no bracket. The prompt's corrected handoff was right and the page followed it: the batch opens after day 773, not at day 736 and not at day 749. **REVIEWED AND REPAIRED — eighteen findings closed, eight of them must-fix. No beat, day-table position, title, turn, midpoint, climax, irreversible decision, bracket or stage moved. Full record in `state/continuity.md` under *Volume 6 Batch 0004 (ch 346–360) — REVIEW REPAIR*; the per-chapter entries below describe the repaired text.** Full day table, money table and construction budget in `state/continuity.md` under *Established facts, Volume 6 Batch 0004 (ch 346–360) — STANDING AND REFUSING TO STAND* and *Construction budget — Volume 6 Batch 0004 (ch 346–360)*.

- **Ch 346 — *The Lines On The Wall* (days 774–775, the eleventh and twelfth).** The board has **four lines** and five more come down out of the same place over the next two months, and after those the yard goes quiet until the season turns. **The structural answer, first mouth, the man behind the stack, four sentences and then he goes back in:** *there is a line out west of this yard, about nine miles, it is walked and it is kept and there are men on it and they are paid by the day and their families are on it, and nobody east of here has ever needed one thing out of it.* She has wanted **a line on that board her hand cannot do** since a listing seven weeks back, and the board does not know what her hand is and never will. **Ten weeks of gate at a third of a reach is seventy coppers and bread at two a day is a hundred and forty.** She takes the one line the board prices by weight — *footing at the far gully, two days, one box*, bank of stone and not clay — because it is the last thing on nine days of road that pays by what it weighs. *Emotion: dread with a shape in it, and the wanting is for a thing to be wrong.*

- **Ch 347 — *Nine Feet Off The Road* (days 776–777, the thirteenth and fourteenth) — THE HUNTER'S ONLY CARD IN THE BATCH.** Nine feet off the cart track, notebook shut, buys nothing, asks nothing. **She asks him, for the first time in five months, and the question is *what is a room with a date on it*.** He answers once, flatly: yes; four rooms on that ground he has been in, two of them shut, both on the last day of a month, neither of them because of anything he wrote. Then, unasked, four lines a day, nine hundred pages nobody reads, a bundle that goes in with the district at the end of a month, **and his paper is still in with the district and a writ is not issued to a field agent, and he is still here and will not come inside nine feet again unless she puts him there. His request is still pending. The line's own people still do not know she exists.** **She wanted him to be angry with her and he was not, and there was no handle on him anywhere**, and what she walked out with instead was the ordinary shape of the only thing that might carry a paper nine days of road — a bundle a man puts in at the end of a month that he has never seen anybody take. *Emotion: the specific loneliness of being the only one carrying it.*

- **Ch 348 — *The Room With A Date In It* (days 778–780, the fifteenth to the seventeenth).** The fourth line at the foot of the strip, the only one of the four in the fine hand she cannot read: **the doors of that room shut on the twenty-eighth of a month.** She works out which one — **the twenty-eighth of this month, thirteen days off**, then twelve by the time she says it out loud. **The strip came down on the seventeenth of a month four weeks back and the twenty-eighth of that month was eleven days after it and went past seventeen days ago while she stood on flat ground.** Nine days of road there and nine days back on bread, at four fingers, with a bar in one hand, **to learn in a room what a holding is.** *(The first draft had the bar taking two hands here, eight chapters before Golden Core 8 takes it. Repaired.)* She says the number first and then the thing, the way she has done every time for four years. **He gives her two sentences, present tense, and they are about himself and not advice:** *I was in a room like that. There was a table and a man with a brush, and he did not look up when he finished the figure.* Then the first day on the footing — the fifteenth: five feet in, four feet deep, eleven feet of bank on the low side, **a footing is the bank you put back and you put it back with your hands and a bar and in the order the bank will stand in** — and the **seventeenth on the low side**, which is the other half of the job: the face off and piled for two days, and what is left is putting the stone back where it came out of, eleven times up before the light went, breath five at the third count and four on the crest, every one of the eleven. *(The chapter once ran the first day twice, about 150 words apart, in the same hours. The second pass is now the seventeenth and a different day.)* *Emotion: dread, and a want — she wanted to arrive before the twenty-eighth and she cannot, and the wanting does not change the road.*

- **Ch 349 — *Lift, Set Early, Move By Hand* (days 781–783, the eighteenth to the twentieth) — THE METHOD, STATED ONCE.** **The middle bank was lighter stone and tighter footing and the method was never strength; it was that you never waited. Lift with the furnace. Set early. Move by hand.** Learned at a bank nine days west from a woman with a scar across the back of both hands. Eleven feet of stone bank put back a foot at a time in an order that will stand, two days against the board's four. **A box is about four days of the ring and it is the only number on nine days of road that has been going up instead of down.** And standing on the crest at the seventh hour she feels **closer to pleasure than anything she has felt on that ground in five months** — *no one on nine days of road is paying her for this* — **and a bad feeling about the pleasure**, because in about two months the board will be bare and the footing at the far gully is two days on a board going bare inside that. *(The first draft had her know here that this was the last footing she would ever be able to do, and tell the reader when she would find out. Close third does not narrate what she does not know, and it pre-announced ch 352 three chapters early. Repaired.)* The man behind the stack: *that is the way it wants to go back in and not one person on this ground has asked me that about anything in eleven years.* She tells him it is not work, it is a bar in one hand and four fingers on the other. He says that is what good work is. *Emotion: pleasure, and the first time in five months that it is not paid for.*

- **Ch 350 — *What The Quiet Is For* (day 784, the twenty-first).** The four lines read from the bottom up. **The listing man says what quiet is:** when those four are done there is nothing on the board until the season turns — *not because the yard has nothing to do, because no man has put anything down* — **and two men on the yard's books go to the low work, and the low work is cut and put back and it is not footing.** **The structural answer, second mouth, four sentences and nothing shared with the first:** *there is a line out west of this gate about nine miles, it was there before this yard had a board, men go out on it in the morning and their families eat off it, and it is kept by men who are not me.* *Not one line of work has ever come in off it to this yard, and I have been in this gateway eleven years.* *(All four mouths said the same four sentences before the review. See the repair record.)* He is asked what is on the ground on the day after the last line and says: *a gate, and whatever anybody wants to pay for out of a field.* **THE BATCH'S ANGER: she is angry at him for a minute and a quarter on the walk east, and there is nothing wrong with him — he is right, the board is right, the gate is right, and being right has a price list and she is the only person paying any of it.** She stops at the crest of the bank she has just put eleven feet of stone back into and stands on it and counts what it was worth: two days of board pay, two boxes, and the whole of the reason she went to that bank. *Emotion: anger with no target, and then the grief.*

- **Ch 351 — *The Last Heavy Thing* (day 785, the twenty-second — the day before Golden Core 8).** The ditch line in twenty feet and twenty feet back, **the bar good in the one hand the whole of both days**, and then she carries it east and asks a question she has been carrying since the eighteenth: *anything heavier than a footing.* **The ramp.** Dressed stone laid on edge, the heaviest thing on that ground, three men, nine days, **not on the board and not for anybody and paid out of the yard in the autumn by a man who is not him, and he has not told anybody on that gate about it in nine years.** *You are not having it. You know that without me telling you and you asked me because you wanted to hear a man say it out loud so that you could stop asking.* On the walk back she works out that she asked for a line her hand could not do for eleven days out loud, at a post and a gate and a bench, **and she is going to get it in the morning and it is going to cost her the last thing her body can price and there is not one person within nine days of road she is going to be able to tell.** At the second hour she says *I am taking one in the morning* and he gives her two sentences, present tense, about the shape of the last six, and does not say he is sorry and does not say she should not do it. Then her palm on the cold stone, and the arm opening and shutting at the door frame. *Emotion: want, shaped like dread.*

- **Ch 352 — *It Takes Two Hands Now* (day 786, the twenty-third, pinned — GOLDEN CORE 8).** Open ground past the last hedge at the fourth hour with the bar on the grass, after nine minutes of counting her breath on purpose.

  [Ning Yan, past the line, servant-born, under no ceiling any county holds. Golden Core, stage eight, is held.]

  **Bracket 5 of 6. She picks the bar up and it comes up, and she goes up a four-foot bank with four dressed pieces under one arm, and her breath goes to five at the third count the way it has done since ch 342, and at the crest she cannot put the pieces down.** The method was never strength, it was never waiting, and it is stated once, at ch 349: **the bar goes under the stone before the stone comes, the stone comes up on the bar, and the last foot of it goes by hand with the arm open and the fingers flat on it. That is two hands.** *(The chapter once explained all of it a second time in full. It now carries only the deduction.)* She has eight holds in the left and four fingers and a thumb on the right, and the right arm open half the time for four years. **She can carry a bar. She cannot carry a stone and a bar.** Said out loud on open ground where there is not a living thing within half a mile, in two sentences. **The heavy end closes to her for good, and what closes is not the ramp — it is every piece of work on nine days of road measured by what comes out of the ground in weight rather than in length, and nothing on that board pays her for it and nothing on that board could be put her on.** *(The first draft had *there is no listing on that board …* twice in a row, and a narrator line that this would be like that for the rest of her life, eight chapters before ch 360 gave her a reach. Both are out.)* She had asked for a line the board could not give her, for eleven days. **She got one, and it was not on the board.** *Emotion: she asked for it and got exactly what she asked for.*

- **Ch 353 — *The Weight Goes To The Bar* (days 787–790, the twenty-fourth to the twenty-seventh).** **A reach is not a length.** Five coppers a reach is five coppers for what comes out of that cut and goes over that bank, and she can dig and she can put footings out on the flat, and the bar goes in and comes up and the stone goes back in the hole it came from. **Pei is right and says so in four sentences:** *that is a fifth of a reach; you took nine feet out of it and got about four feet over the bank before noon and the rest of it is in the drain and it is not costing me anything to have it in the drain. Five coppers a reach. A fifth of a reach is one. I am not taking it out of what you do, I am taking it out of what you take over the bank.* **One copper a day. Bread at two.** The store five miles off gives her four days on two coppers. Three lines still on the board and all three measured by length and all three of them hers to take. **The structural answer, third mouth, a carter who has driven past the end of the line four hundred times and was never let onto it:** *men go out on it and come back, they are paid by the day, their families are on it, and in nineteen years I have watched that road from this side and not one of them has ever come off it with anything in his hands.* *I do not know what is at the top of it and I am not the man who would find out.* She tries to work out what the six stages have taken and finds the sixth took the bar, which was hiding the rest of it. *Emotion: the plain part about one person, four words long.*

- **Ch 354 — *Nothing Came Down The Road* (days 791–795, the twenty-eighth to the second).** **The first twenty-eighth.** She gets up at the fourth hour out of the habit of five months and there is a fire in the corner of the shed lit by itself. She works a fifth of a reach for one copper and stands at the gate post at the eighth hour and does the sum. **Nothing came down the mill road.** The road does not report on a room. If she had been in it she would have known by the eighth hour; if she had not, she would find out in about six weeks if she ever does, **because a strip comes down every nine or ten days and one in three carries words at the bottom of it.** **She had been carrying a date for four weeks and had thought that carrying it was the same as being on time. It was not the same at all.** The cart goes up on the first with a page fanned open — the seventh tray. **She wanted somebody to come and tell her**, and underneath that she wanted to be wrong about the seventh stage, and **she had asked for the stage herself on open ground in daylight at her own order**, and she lies on the boards and works out that the whole of what she has bought in four years is the right to stand on a piece of ground and be the one who decides. **The chapter closes on twenty-three days to the next one** — 795 to 818 — *which the first draft got wrong under every reading and which contradicted ch 357's own arithmetic three chapters later.* *Emotion: the worst thing in the batch so far, and it is a silence with a date on it.*

- **Ch 355 — *He Will Not Give One* (days 796–801, the third to the eighth) — THE HAND REFUSED ONCE. REFUSED, NOT SPENT.** The third strip in six weeks with words at the bottom of it, and a fifth line under the four — **the holder named is the only holder named, a second holder is to be named under the first, and the naming is to be done by the first holder** — and the first time she can read any of it except the one line at the top that carries her column, because **that line is in the ordinary hand.** A second holder is a second mark under the first so that if one name cannot be produced another can; every book on nine days of road has one for every column and every one of them is one name, and no office on that ground has ever needed a second. She carries it two miles to the shed rather than ask it in a gateway with nine men listening, puts it on the boards, and asks. **He says no in the first word and then gives the whole of it in present tense and never once asks her what she is going to do.** *A second holder is a second person an office can send for, and it would be on that line for as long as the line is there, and so that man at the works, with a cart that goes twice a week, would be on it at the far end of his life and would not know that is what he was standing under.* **And the price of refusing, said by her and agreed by him:** *you are refusing a thing that would make me safer.* **Yes.** *Emotion: not fear — the recognition that the only two people who could have made her safe have both said no out loud, for reasons of their own, and she cannot argue with either of them.*

- **Ch 356 — *The Room With Two Things In It* (days 802–817, the ninth to the twenty-fourth).** **She writes the refusal herself on the back of the strip at the fourth hour** — three lines, eleven minutes a line with the flat of her second finger, every line costing its hour twice over — and it goes back up the road on the next bundle on the twelfth. **Nothing came out of the ground she was standing on; her hands are her hands.** The ditch line in four days and the head line in three against the board's three, **and by the fifteenth the board is bare** — four columns, the ruled space at the left with her name in it, and no line running into any of them. **The listing man is right: it comes back when the season turns and it comes back full, and he will not give her the date and she cannot get another word out of him about it.** One copper a day, bread five days in seven, two boxes of clay off two lines. At the second hour on the twenty-third she asks him **whether he can write** — *no, not since before you were born, and not since the fourth year, and I have not looked for it since about the third year, because a thing you cannot do is not a thing you go looking for.* **On the twenty-fourth she goes up to the counting house with the question she has had ready since the eleventh, and the man with the brush will not let her stand there with it: he puts two sheets on the top step and comes back down.** One has her column on it in a hand she can read from nine feet. **The other has two columns on it, and under the days are two lines with nothing at the other end of the rule, in the hand she has never been able to read.** *Emotion: the last want of the batch, in its first shape — she wanted to know whether anything is findable and she is about to find out that she is findable twice.*

- **Ch 357 — *Copied Upward In A Fine Hand* (day 818, the twenty-fifth, pinned — GOLDEN CORE 9 AND THE FINDING).** Open ground at the fourth hour, and **she counts back from Golden Core eight — the twenty-third of a month a month back — and gets thirty-two days, not thirty-seven, twice, and does not know what to make of it.** *(The first draft counted back from Golden Core seven, which makes thirty-two impossible. Repaired.)*

  [Ning Yan, past the line, servant-born, under no ceiling any county holds. Golden Core, stage nine, is held.]

  **Bracket 6 of 6, the last in the volume. The furnace finished at the seventh hour: three handfuls of ash did not bring it up and did not put it out, and a lump out of a box went in at the low end and it came up against the brick and stood there about nine feet high, and it is hers.** **And then the finding, in the ordinary course, at the end of the month, on the copying floor, from a correct man with a brush.** He copies down. Everything comes up that stair and is read against everything else that went up it, and **he has never in eleven years copied a thing upward out of that room and is not the man who could** — and the second sheet came down on the bundle on the twenty-second **already written**, in a hand finer than his, with her days on the left and under them the materials account against the mill and the holding, **both with nothing at the other end of the rule.** She asked for it at a gate four months ago to buy clay. **It had gone four hundred yards further than she had asked for it to.** *She is a person two offices will vouch for, and she found out on the last step of six stages she had taken in seven months what the second of those two vouchings was copied into. She cannot be un-entered twice over.* **The whole of what asking in daylight bought her is that she is standing at the bottom of the stair finding out, instead of a person who finds out from a strip about three weeks after it happens.** The structural answer, fourth mouth, four sentences and nothing shared with the other three, and he does not know he is saying it: *there is a line out west of this building about nine miles, it is kept, and what is on it is paid for by the day out of a place I have never seen, and every month-end the ordinary traffic of it comes past this floor on a cart and is read against other things.* **You are not the first person on this floor to be a person a road went past, and you will not be the last, and I have been the man who copies it for eleven years.** *Emotion: the flat one — she wanted to see the end of that fine hand before anybody else did and the hand saw her first.*

- **Ch 358 — *She Says No At The Counter* (days 819–820, the twenty-sixth and the twenty-seventh) — THE SENDING REFUSED, ENTERED AND STANDING.** **The twenty-eighth is two days off** — the first draft said the thirtieth and contradicted the chapter's own date three times over. ** **The man behind the counter tells her the whole of it in the ordinary voice** — her line came back down the road on the twenty-second and was read, it is not his to send back, he has a counter and a book and six men and a stove and a line that has to be produced in a room on the twenty-eighth, and **she is the only person on nine days of road that has got a road to that building** — and says he will ask one question in that room in front of those six and will write down what she says either way. *Are you going to carry the holder in.* **She said her name first, the way she has said it at a trestle and at a gate and at a bench. Ning Yan, of Lowwater. No.** *The holder is not presented. I am not carrying him to that room. And I will not stand in one and say that I have.* **He writes, at the left of the page in the margin, against the day: “Ning Yan, of Lowwater. The holder is not presented. Refused at the counter. The twenty-sixth.”** Fifteen words, read back to himself once without being said out loud. *(Set in sentence case inside quotation marks; it stood as a bare all-caps line, which reads as a panel.)* He does not write why, and says why he does not. **Three things in that book now with nothing at the other end of the rule — the materials account, the holding, and her refusal — and it was already going up four times as it is, and it goes up on the first cart that night with a refusal in it that was not in it on the first of the month, and she did that herself, and there is no hand in that building that can take it out.** **And the reason she went up there, said on the three-mile walk back: she had not gone to protect the line, she had gone because there was a date and it was two days off and there was no one else on nine days of road who could say anything about it.** Ten weeks at her ribs for that fourth line — the strip came down on day 750 and this is day 819 — **and six weeks ago eleven feet off a man with a notebook in ten inches of wet grass, told of four rooms on ground he had been in and two that shut, and not told what one of them looked like and not able to ask.** *(The first draft put her inside that room three weeks ago and called the Hunter the only person who had ever been in one. She has never been in it; the refusal exists because she cannot produce him.)* *Emotion: the batch's irreversible act. It is not fear. She is eighteen, and everything that ever touched her on nine days of road has been right, and she has just said no to the last one of them in a room with a stove.*

- **Ch 359 — *A Book Under A Ladder* (days 821–840, the twenty-eighth to the seventeenth) — WHAT BREAKING COST.** **The break is ch 344's and this is the bill and the two chapters do not say the other.** The second twenty-eighth passes with nothing on the road. The tray goes up on the first with the cord over a page fanned open — the seventh. **On the ninth, about the eleventh hour — eight days after the first cart had gone up, not nine — a tray comes back down the mill road on the cart that takes the empties up.** A cord is still round it and the book will not shut. **The man behind the counter could not keep it on a counter and could not send it up a fifth time, and he was right, and that is the bill.** A keeper of about fifty-five who has kept that board nine years has never in nine years had a book come down the road. He asks *what do I do with a book.* **The bill is said in a room with a person** — a low building at the back of the board with a bench in it — **and she tells him: a furnace no office on nine days of road built, a holder not one person in the world can produce, and a refusal with her name at the left of it.** He pastes the strip in anyway, because that is what the board is for, **and then he says the other half, which is new: the board is for the strips.** The book goes under the ladder on a stone, because the stone is the driest thing he has, and it stands there. **No office that reads that fine hand is ever going to know where it is**, and the only man holding it cannot read it and will not tell anybody, because there is nobody to tell. *Emotion: the plain part about one person, and it is a woman in a shed a mile up that road who cleared some work off a board because she wanted clay.*

- **Ch 360 — *The Length Of A Reach* (days 845–848, the twenty-second to the twenty-fifth) — NASCENT SOUL 1.
