NO BRACKET.** The board has been bare for about a month, the season has not turned, the gate pays one copper, and she goes out past the mill at the seventh hour **with no bar**, because the bar takes two hands and it has stood against the fence of the shed since the twenty-third of the month before last. A gang of four men two hundred yards off. She puts her hand flat on a stone she has not touched. **It comes.** Not up. Along, at the pace her hand goes, the length of her own arm, and it stops with her hand on it and she puts it down where it stopped. **The hole it was in is the shape the stone was in and there is no dust around the far side of it.** **Nobody hears it: four men nine feet off do not stop, and that is correct of them, because a stone that goes four feet and stops is a thing a man with a bar can do.** **Nothing is said.** No bracket, no announcement, no voice. **And she is surprised by nobody in the room where it happens, and the surprise does not come back** — where he is, in the room with the floor and the table and the two shut doors, **nothing at all happened, because she had not put this there**, and she had been able to put everything else there for four years. She goes back on the twenty-fourth and the twenty-fifth and it does not repeat, and the not being disappointed was the other half of the cost. **On the twenty-fifth a carter brings her a strip off the bundle because her name is at the top of it, and she can read the ordinary half and cannot read four or five lines in the fine hand — and she does not need to, because there is a date on that strip and she knows the date. **The fine hand has put a word at the head of those four or five lines and she cannot read a letter of it, and she knows that word too: the one after Golden Core, Nascent Soul, the fifth, a name she has had since before she could count to nine, and she went into it three days ago on a bank behind a mill with four men two hundred yards off and nothing said out loud.**** *(The realm's name was on no page in the manuscript before this. It is a name on a page here and not a panel: ch 360 still carries no bracket, and the silence when the stone moves is unbroken.)* *The hand can read what happened. She cannot read the hand.* Nine days of road behind her, a stair, a floor, nine men, a tower with a light in it. Everything that ever happened to her inside that boundary was announced, out loud, to nine people who had to write it down before she finished saying it. **She will not walk it again** — said in her own act, without a farewell to anybody. *Emotion: not surprise, and the absence of it, and the first thing in five months she has not been able to pay for and cannot buy again.*

---

## Volume 6 closed — audited

**`outline/volume-06.md` walked line by line against ch 299–360. Sixteen faults repaired across seventeen chapter
files, seven findings recorded and not repaired, the seventeen-item continuity list walked one at a time, and the
protagonist answered in writing. File set after the close repairs: 95,081 words of body prose over sixty-two
chapters (94,946 before them), by batch 24,529 · 23,975 · 25,027 · 21,550 — **and 95,110 by batch
24,529 · 23,975 · 25,056 · 21,550 after the close's own review repaired three more chapters and re-derived
every figure. No beat, day, title, turn, bracket or stage moved.**

**The goal, fixed by `outline/series.md` §9 — *In this volume, Ning Yan must survive what is on the other side, or
the crossing was a death and not an escape* — is paid.** The far side is ordinary farmed country with offices in it
and none of them an enemy; the pressure is that being legible has a price list and she is the one paying it; she is
entered twice and on the last step finds out the second vouching was copied upward in a hand she has never seen;
she refuses a sending out loud, in daylight, with her name said, and it is entered and standing in a building she
cannot reach; she breaks the closed-season box circuit and the bill is a book of hers on a stone under a keeper's
ladder at a post; and she reaches Nascent Soul 1 by hand and not by sound, on a bank, where an office writes it
down afterward because the hand can read what happened. **The cost of surviving is stated in the chapters and not
in a plan**: the heavy end closed for good, the gate at one copper a day, a bare board, a refusal she cannot take
back, and a reach that happened once.

**Four pressures, four batches, no repeat:** reading and being read · work without hire · sending and being sent
for · standing and refusing to stand. **The volume took the first rung of *Breaking* and broke one thing (the
closed-season box circuit, ch 344) and refused one thing (the sending, ch 358). Nothing opened the Wall and no
Order appeared.**

**Six bracketed layer lines in sixty-two chapters**, at ch 310, 321, 332, 342, 352 and 357, and **none at ch 360**:
Golden Core 4 (day 638) · 5 (674) · 6 (712) · 7 (749) · 8 (786) · 9 (818), and Nascent Soul 1 (845) entered by
hand. Seven costs, each named once and each a different thing, and **ch 357's is the only one that is not a thing
her body lost.** No tribulation anywhere in the volume.

**Yuan: Hand spent twice (ch 325, ch 338) and refused once out loud (ch 355) and not spent.** His unentered state
and the date of the morning he died are carried and not given back; his unnamed state went at ch 338, named in
advance. **Ledger never ordered, held, priced, refused or thought about** — `grep -i Ledger` returns zero across
the volume after two close repairs. Merit settles twice and neither time on a death (ch 316, ch 320) and not at all
in Batch 0004. **No figure of what is left of him is stated on any page in ch 299–360.**

**The structural answer is begun in seven mouths — ch 331, 335, 337, 346, 350, 353 and 357 — and the tenth part is
not given and nothing proves the crews wrong.** *(Corrected at the close audit: the record previously said four
mouths in Batch 0003 at ch 331, 335, 337 **and 345**; ch 345's mouth says nothing about the line west. There are
seven, not eight.)*

**The protagonist, answered in writing.** He moved a cart's turning through her column at ch 325 so a box would go
on a week early. He let an office's hand address him as the holder of her column at ch 338 so her furnace hours
would hold without her, at the price of his unnamed state, named in advance. He was asked for a second holder at
ch 355 and said no out loud, and was not spent, and the price of refusing was that he was refusing a thing that
would have made her safer — she said so and he agreed. **Every one of those was paid for out of her.** He is now
readable without her and findable, held and sendable, and **being sent for is a thing that can happen to him in
principle and Volume 6 neither resolves it nor has it happen off-page.** The closing position is the one the ending
needs: **a man the reader has seen in a room, in his own frame, twice.**

**Full table, every repair and the hand-off: `state/current.md` under `## Volume 6 close audit — OPENED` and `##
Volume 6 close audit — CLOSED`. Figures re-derived from the chapters: `state/continuity.md` under `## Volume 6 close
— repairs`. Next phase: **the Volume 7 planning phase**, which writes `outline/volume-07.md`.**

---

## Volume 7 planned — *Stronger Things*, ch 361–420

**`outline/volume-07.md` was written by the Volume 7 planning phase. No Volume 7 chapter exists. This block is the
plan, not a summary of prose, and it is here so that the Volume 7 drafting phases and the Volume 7 close have a
single place to walk against.**

### The shape of it

Sixty chapters, day 849 to day 1,090, four batches of 14 / 15 / 15 / 16, six Nascent Soul stages, six bracketed layer
lines. Goal fixed by `outline/series.md` §9 and not re-planned: *In this volume, Ning Yan must survive being the
weakest person in every room she walks into, or she learns that strength was never what she was taken for.*

### The four batches

| Batch | Chapters | Days | Pressure | Midpoint | Climax | Irreversible step |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 0001 | 361–374 (14) | 849–899 | **rooms bigger than she is** | ch 368 — the yard of nine men where she is the smallest thing in it | ch 371 — Nascent Soul 2, and thirteen days of a shed with a fire in it that does not come up | the first stage taken in the open with people watching (ch 371) |
| 0002 | 375–389 (15) | 900–950 | **what she is worth in a room** | ch 383 — Nascent Soul 3, and a false line can no longer be entered | ch 388 — she says a name out loud and asks | **she asks, and cannot un-ask** |
| 0003 | 390–404 (15) | 951–1,020 | **the reach on purpose** | ch 394 — Nascent Soul 4, and it does not go back | ch 401–402 — the third frame scene and the break | **the answer goes up, and she can never refuse a reading again** |
| 0004 | 405–420 (16) | 1,021–1,090 | **what it costs to be right** | ch 412 — Nascent Soul 6, and the bare board becomes the rest of the run | ch 417 the refusal · ch 419 the lift and the Hand refused · ch 420 Nascent Soul 7 and the column read out loud | **she declines to know what the fine hand says about her, forever, on purpose** |

### The six stages, and the six costs, each named once

- **Nascent Soul 2 · ch 371 · day 886 · the third of a month · 41 days after NS 1** — **the furnace will not light
  itself and she cannot light it alone.**
- **Nascent Soul 3 · ch 383 · day 924 · the eleventh · 38 days** — **her hand stops being able to lie.**
- **Nascent Soul 4 · ch 394 · day 960 · the seventeenth · 36 days** — **a thing she reaches cannot be put back where
  it was.**
- **Nascent Soul 5 · ch 404 · day 999 · the twenty-sixth · 39 days** — **she cannot be given anything.**
- **Nascent Soul 6 · ch 412 · day 1,037 · the fourth · 38 days** — **she cannot be paid.**
- **Nascent Soul 7 · ch 420 · day 1,076 · the thirteenth · 39 days** — **the reach will not go out of a room she has
  not been asked into.**

### The three new people, and the one that stays unnamed

**Rao** keeps the columns on the third floor, sixteen years, and is the midpoint. **Dai** can read the fine hand and
offers once and is refused once. **Geng** runs the lifting floor, is strong and correct, and his floor is a block
short for a week. **The keeper with the paste pot stays unnamed**, as in Volume 6, and carries the heaviest scene.

### The structural answer, six mouths

ch 365 (a carter) · ch 379 (a gate man) · ch 391 (Rao) · ch 398 (a woman who drives clay) · ch 409 (a copying man
below Rao) · ch 415 (Geng). **Four or five sentences each, never as a speech. The tenth part is not given and nothing
proves the crews wrong.**

### The hand-off to Volume 8

**Not one word of the Order is on Volume 7's page.** The question Volume 7 opens onto it: *the last strip went east
and did not come back, she has never been east of here, and something in a country that big has been sending for
her by name since before she knew she was being read — what happens to the first person who comes for the reader.*

**Figures:** 95,110 words of body prose stand on the page for Volume 6 (24,529 · 23,975 · 25,056 · 21,550 by batch),
360 `#` headings, zero collisions. **Volume 7 contributes zero words and zero headings until it is drafted.**

## Volume 7 Batch 0001 — *Stronger Things*, ch 361–374, day 849–899 — WRITTEN

**Rooms bigger than she is.** 23,101 words of body prose over fourteen chapters, average ~1,650. One bracketed layer
line, at ch 371, which is bracket 1 of Volume 7's six in sixty. Full record in `state/continuity.md` under
***Established facts, Volume 7 Batch 0001 (ch 361–374) — ROOMS BIGGER THAN SHE IS***.

| Ch | Day | Month-day | Title | Summary |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 361 | **849** | the 26th | The Beginning Of A Habit | The morning after ch 360, at the gate, with a strip folded in two inside her coat that came down on the twenty-fifth. **The bar takes two hands, so the gate now works on a short iron borrowed out of the gate house at dawn and returned before she leaves, and the reach has been a fifth since ch 353 because of it.** She walks three miles to a board that has been bare for about a month and notices she has been doing it four days running with nothing to look for, and does not stop. **The turn: nothing has come for her and nothing is going to**, and the only thing that comes instead is a strip in a hand she cannot read. Wants somebody to come; says out loud on the walk west that she would have gone back if they had. Yuan silent. |
| 362 | 850–851 | the 27th–28th | What A Month Comes To | **A month is thirty coppers.** She does the whole of it standing up in a gateway on the twenty-seventh: bread twenty, a third of a sack three, seven over. A third of a sack is three of the seven and four coppers is two days of bread. The store is five miles west and its woman has never asked her anything. She asks the man who keeps the list whether the board is the only place work comes from and is told no, and then told that a yard does not put a line on a board for a woman it does not have on its books, and that a book she is on is a book somebody reads. **Yuan, present tense, buys nothing: *Thirty days. You have counted them three times and you counted the paper wrong on one of them.*** She counted the third sum wrong because she left out the paper, which is not money and never was. |
| 363 | 851 | the 28th | Eleven Years Of Copying Down | The copying floor and the counter, **the man with the brush appearing once in this batch**. She asks him to read the four lines in the middle of her strip; he cannot read that hand and never has and has looked for eleven years. **A thing in a hand she cannot read gets written down by somebody and goes up a stair and comes down a road and gets set against another thing, and the answer to that does not come down.** The counter cannot read it either and will not send it back down in his hand. He tells her what the end of a month is: everything that comes into the room in thirty days goes up the mill road at the sixth hour with a cord round it, shutting or not, **including the refusal she gave a month ago in front of nine men.** She spent her question a month and got the same answer for the third time. |
| 364 | 852–853 | the 29th–30th | Read Out Of The Room | **The mechanism, shown before it is named.** At the works gateway on the twenty-ninth a man in a yard's coat is read one line out loud, with the weight in it, and says the figures back from memory and carries a slip east. **The reading has left the room and gone to a person.** Then on the thirtieth the same man is read all eleven lines at once and takes four slips — **a reading can make a crowd.** She works out on the walk west why she had not seen it in six months: every reading that had ever touched her had a person inside it, and this one had none. Yuan, present tense, three sentences, buys nothing: *Then stop asking the question and watch the thing.* She decides to be standing in that yard at the sixth hour on the first of a month, eleven days' walk out of the ninth hour, with a week's bread. |
| 365 | 853–855 | the 30th, the 1st, the 2nd | Where The Cart Goes | **The first thing that has ever gone east on that ground.** At ten minutes past the sixth hour on the first, the tray is corded and the mill cart goes up the mill road past the elms; and a strip about as wide as a hand, folded in two with a stone on it, goes on the tail-board of the second cart and out of the yard east. She watches it until it is a shape on the flat. The counter's boy is sure it goes up the mill road. An east carter with a chain over the cart bed tells her the road is four miles to the turn and five more and then the long road — **and mouth one of Volume 7's six: he has been turned back at the end of it four hundred times, turning back is the ordinary end of the road, turning back is what people pay for, they pay for the going out and they pay for the stopping, and there is no part in the middle.** Asked whether it comes back: no, and it will be on the cart that takes the empties up, and there is a different man on that one. |
| 366 | 856–864 | the 3rd–11th | What Did Not Come Back | The east cart comes back in **empty** behind a load of timber, and she stands at the gate post looking at a bare tail-board. **The mechanism in four lines: a strip that goes up the mill road comes back on the ninth with the empty trays, and a strip that goes east does not, because that cart does not go east.** Four ordinary days at a fifth of a reach. On the ninth the empty-tray cart comes down with four nested trays, a rope and a stone, **and nothing else at all**, and she stands nine feet off and watches it go past the elms. The plain part about one person: she is eighteen and cannot read the hand, and the answer came back as less and she wants to walk nine miles east and there is not one thing she could do at either end. Pei is right about the drain going shallower and says so at the eighth hour on the eleventh. **The season turns on the eleventh.** |
| 367 | 864 | the 11th | What Reading Is Worth | **The board comes back full — eleven lines, and not one of them says a length.** Every line is dressed stone for a yard east, priced by the ton. She can take none, and she works out standing in the gateway why a length line is not hers either: it goes to whoever is at the wall when a man's hand is on it, and there are nine men in that yard. Then the listing man reads the board out loud to a man from a yard and hands him four slips, and she watches a reading make a crowd. She asks whether he will read it for money. **The reading is not a trade, is not on anybody's books and is not paid, and a board has one man who reads it.** He tells her he has known she could read a column since the day she stood in that gateway and asked to be put in an account, and that knowing it is not work. She is angry at him for two minutes and he is right about all of it, and she writes down with the flat of her second finger the whole of what she can do: a fifth of a reach, cutting by length, reading — **and there is nothing she can put against the third one.** |
| 368 | 867–869 | the 14th–16th | **The Ninth Man** — the batch midpoint | **The yard at the turn four miles east: brick and timber, a covered bay, a bar against the end post, nine men on a board where four will do.** She is the smallest thing in the bay and a man with a rule in his hand takes her head to the boots in about two seconds, most of it the right arm. **He asks her for a thing she can do and then a thing she cannot, and he is right about both.** The length is a hedge, four hundred yards at the east end, and it is on his board and is not hers — no name of hers goes anywhere in that yard — and she gets the foot of it up wrong and does the last hundred yards again in the dark. Paid in a cup of something hot from a woman who has never asked her name. The sill is two hundred and something of dressed stone, eleven pieces to the tonne, and **he had worked out which of his nine she was at the second hour yesterday and it is the one that is not on the board, and he never intended to ask twice.** She wanted him to make her ask twice and he did not. **The chapter's finding: a yard prices a body and every office she has been entered in has her column in it, and nowhere on nine days of road has her arm on a line of her own.** |
| 369 | 873–875 | the 20th–22nd | What A Room Is For | The hedge is closed off the board — done, checked, off — and she walked four miles to find out whether a board keeps the thing you did. The man with the rule pushes a barrow-load of her own gate clay back at her: he has four tons coming off that bank every day and a man paid to be on it, and he will not take clay off a woman at a gate. Nine men take no notice of her the second morning, and that is the point: **she has been priced, and it took about two seconds with a rule in a hand.** Then, nine feet off the cart track, the **Hunter**, notebook shut, buys nothing, asks nothing, and she asks him — **what a room on this ground is for** — and he answers in the ordinary voice and at no cost to either of them: **a room is where a thing is put so that four people can agree about it later.** Then: still here, still buying nothing, not going to tell her why, and she is not going to ask. She works it out on the walk home: **she had been the thing in every room she had walked into for six months and had been getting it wrong.** Yuan, two sentences, the arrangement. |
| 370 | 884–885 | the 1st–2nd | The Last Day It Lit Itself | **Merit settles once, on a listed clearing, not on a death** — the hedge, closed and checked and off on the twentieth, done with her hands and off nobody's book. **The share is six days and four hours and he says it out loud twice before she spends it**, because she is going to spend it before she has had a night with it and he would rather she heard it. She spends it on nothing; it goes out of the ground under her and does not come back. She puts her hand on the ring to say goodnight to it, and it is warm. **Then the action card: the second of a month, a bank a mile and a quarter east of the mill, nine men on a face about sixty feet long.** A bar comes out of the face with four pieces of the heavy stone on it and comes down the low side, and she is on the low side filling a barrow. About a ton. **She lifts — the thing she learned at a bank nine days west in three words — and it comes up out of the ground under her ribs and goes under what is on top of her, and the bar stops about a finger's width** and two men take the rest. She stands up whole, and puts her hands out in front of her, **and they are cold.** They had been warm for four months. |
| 371 | **886** | the 3rd | **NASCENT SOUL 2** — What Two Men Saw From The Bank | The fire does not come up at the fourth hour and will not come up with clay, with ash or with her hands flat on the brick. At the seventh hour, out on the flat past the last hedge, **on the high side of a bank with nine men on it and two of them nine feet off**, the stage comes out of the ground she is standing on. [Ning Yan, past the line, servant-born, under no ceiling any county holds. Nascent Soul, stage two, is held.] **Forty-one days after Nascent Soul 1, the longest interval in the volume**, and the chapter says why: the board was bare, the season had not turned, and a stage needs material she had no way to pay for. Nothing goes out of her body and the bill comes four hours later in the shed: she tries it nine times over three hours and it comes up a finger's width and stops every time. **Cost, named once: the furnace will not light itself and she cannot light it alone. A stage cannot be built by one pair of hands.** And the part that cannot be put back: **two men looked at her, and they will say it to the next man who stands on that high side, and there is no book inside nine days of road that can be un-corded.** This batch's irreversible step. |
| 372 | 887–891 | the 4th–8th | The Ring Stays Cold | Thirteen days begin. The fire does not come up on any of the first four, and by the eighth she has stopped expecting it, which is worse, because a thing you expect and do not get is a thing you get up for. **The whole of a day in eleven hours of walking and four hours of standing**: a fifth of a reach, one copper, the store five miles west, the works three miles east with a full board, a length at the yard three days in seven, and one mile and a quarter of flat she does not go out onto. **Half a box of clay is four days of the ring and there is no ring**, so the clay is a barrow against a wall. **Her hands are cold, and the flat stone by the door is cold, and for the first time in four years the two are the same temperature and it tells her nothing.** Four things she could have done about it and does not do — the counter, the yard, her own book off the stone under the keeper's ladder, and asking. The chapter says the reason out loud on the fifth: a thing that comes into a room gets put in a room so that four people can agree about it later, and she cannot read the hand. |
| 373 | 892–893 | the 9th–10th | Two Lines Shorter | She carries half a box of her own gate clay to the yard in a barrow and is told no, correctly: he has four tons coming off that bank and a man paid to be on it. Then, at the second hour on the tenth, **the man with the brush has been waiting since the sixth to give her something before she spends her question.** The cross-reference: her column set against the other book once a month, and this month the column in the fine hand has **two lines at the foot of it where it had four for four months.** He cannot read the hand and refuses to guess at what two means. **She works it out at the bottom of that stair: something went east on the first at a quarter past the sixth hour and something came back, and it came back the other way — down the mill road on the ordinary monthly copy — with two lines off it.** It did not come back on the cart. She does not ask her question. She asks instead whether he will set her column against it again next month, and he says that is the cross-reference and it is not done for her. **She is a person two offices vouch for and she has never once been able to read what she has been vouched for.** |
| 374 | 897–899 | the 14th–16th | What She Was Going To Ask | **Thirteen days of a ring that comes up about a finger's width and stops, counted twice with the flat of her second finger.** The whole of what she did with them: thirteen coppers, bread for five days at a time, thirty-nine miles each way, a length six days at the yard and a cup of something hot six days, six days at a full board, four barrow-loads of clay against the outside wall, one trip up the mill road, and nothing spent. **Pei, at the eighth hour, is right about the drain going from four feet to three and a half and says so, and it is the one thing a man at a gate is allowed to say.** The plain part about one person, and the chapter's line: **A stage cannot be built by one pair of hands, and it took four months of that fire coming up on its own to teach her so.** She works out six people she could name and every one of them somebody she would not ask — the rule man, the counter, the listing man, the counter on the mill road, the man with the brush, the gate man, the carter. **And the seventh: the man with the paste pot at a post on the mill road, who cannot read her book and would not tell anybody, and the counting house is two miles short of his own ladder on a road she walks four or five times a month.** She says the thing out loud at the second hour — that she has to put a person in that room and say it and mean it — and then says she has not said it to anybody, and does not say it to a man in a gateway, or a man with a bench, or the layer. |
